. To formulate this we first need an appropriate notion of measurable maps taking values in a space of bounded linear operators. This can be found in Hille and Phillips
[
48
]
(Definition 3.5.4, the subsequent remark applied for
\sigma-
finite measure spaces, and Definition 3.5.5), and we record it now in the second item in the definition below.

Definition 3.2

(Some notions of measurability)
.

Let
X
be a complete and
\sigma
-finite measure space.

(1)

Bochner measurability:
Let
Y
be a Banach space. We say that a function
g:X\to Y
is Bochner measurable if it is the almost everywhere limit of a sequence of finitely-valued measurable (simple) functions.

(2)

Operator-valued strong measurability:
Let
V_{0},V_{1}
be Banach spaces over
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
. We say that a function
f:X\to\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1})
is strongly measurable if for all
v\in V_{0}
we have that the map
fh:X\to V_{1}
is Bochner measurable.

Next we record some essential properties of this notion of measurability.

Theorem 3.3

(Properties of operator-valued strongly measurable functions)
.

Let
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
be separable Hilbert spaces over
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
and
X
be a complete and
\sigma
-finite measure space. Let
f:X\to\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1})
.
Then the following hold.

(1)

f
is strongly measurable in the sense of the second item of Definition
3.2
if and only if for all
v_{0}\in V_{0}
and
v_{1}\in V_{1}
we have that
\langle fv_{0},v_{1}\rangle:X\to\mathbb{F}
is measurable.

(2)

If
f
is strongly measurable and
g:X\to V_{0}
is Bochner measurable in the sense of the first item of Definition
3.2
, then
fg:X\to V_{1}
is also Bochner measurable.

(3)

If
f
is strongly measurable and
g:X\to\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1})
is such that
f=g
almost everywhere, then
g
is strongly measurable.

Proof.

The first item follows from Theorem 3.5.5 in
[
48
]
. To prove the second item, we note that Theorem 3.5.4 in
[
48
]
shows that if
\psi:X\to V_{0}
is simple, then
f\psi:X\to V_{1}
is Bochner measurable. Thus,
fg
is the almost everywhere limit of a sequence of Bochner measurable functions and is then measurable, again by Theorem 3.5.4 in
[
48
]
. The third item follows by noting that if
h\in V_{0}
, then
f(x)h=g(x)h
for almost every
x\in X
, and hence
g(\cdot)h
is Bochner measurable.
∎

The notion of operator-valued strong measurability leads us to the following space of essentially bounded functions (see Blasco and van Neerven
[
24
]
for the generalization to
p<\infty
and applications in the study of multiplication operators between vector-valued Lebesgue spaces).

Definition 3.4

(Space of essentially bounded and strongly measurable operator-valued functions)
.

Let
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
be separable Hilbert spaces over
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
and
X
be a complete and
\sigma
-finite a measure space. We define

(3.2)

L^{\infty}_{\ast}(X;\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1}))\\
=\{{[f]\;|\;f:X\to\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1})\text{ is strongly measurable and essentially bounded}}\}

where
[f]
denotes the usual equivalence class formed via almost everywhere equality. Note, though, that as per usual we will dispense with the equivalence class notation in what follows. For
f\in L^{\infty}_{\ast}(X;\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1}))
we write

(3.3)

\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\ast}\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1})}=\esssup_{x\in X}\left\lVert f(x)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1})}=\inf\{{C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\;:\;\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1})}\leqslant C\text{ a.e.}}\}.

We emphasize that although the notion of almost everywhere is used in the definition of the essential supremum, it does not actually require the map
f
to be measurable; consequently, the essential supremum is well-defined even on the space of strongly measurable
\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1})
-valued maps.

We can now state and prove our first infinite dimensional generalization of Theorem
3.1
. To the best of our knowledge, the following result does not appear in the literature.

Theorem 3.5

(Translation commuting linear maps, infinite dimensional case I)
.

Suppose that
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
are separable infinite dimensional Hilbert spaces over
\mathbb{C}
and that

(3.4)

T:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{0})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{1})

is a bounded linear map. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

T
commutes with translations in the sense that
(Tf)(\cdot+h)=T(f(\cdot+h))
for all
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{0})
.

(2)

There exists
m\in L^{\infty}_{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1}))
such that

(3.5)

Tf=\mathscr{F}^{-1}[m\mathscr{F}[f]]\text{ for every }f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{0}).

In either case, we have the equality

(3.6)

\lVert m\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\ast}\mathcal{L}(V_{0},V_{1})}=\lVert T\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}V_{0};L^{2}V_{1})}.

Proof.

If
T
is given by (
3.5
), then it is a trivial matter to verify that it commutes with translations, so we only need to prove the converse and (
3.6
). We begin with the proof of the latter, assuming that
T=m(D)
for
m\in L^{\infty}_{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1}))
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{0})
. Since for any null set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus E
we have
\lVert m(\xi)\mathscr{F}[f](\xi)\rVert_{V_{1}}\leqslant\sup_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus E}\lVert m\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1})}\lVert\mathscr{F}[f](\xi)\rVert_{V_{0}}
, we are free to integrate the square of this, take the infimum over such
E
, and apply Plancherel’s theorem to deduce that
\lVert Tf\rVert_{L^{2}V_{1}}^{2}=\lVert mf\rVert_{L^{2}V_{1}}^{2}\leqslant\lVert m\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\ast}}^{2}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}V_{0}}^{2}
. Thus,
\lVert T\rVert\leqslant\lVert m\rVert
.

For the opposite inequality, we let
\varphi_{\lambda}=\mathds{1}_{B(0,\lambda)}/\sqrt{\left|B(0,\lambda)\right|}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
. Then for any
x\in V_{0}
,
\xi_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\lambda>0
we have that

(3.7)

\frac{1}{\left|B(0,\lambda)\right|}\int_{B(\xi_{0},\lambda)}\lVert m(\xi)x\rVert_{V_{1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert m(\xi)x\rVert_{V_{1}}^{2}|\varphi_{\lambda}(\xi-\xi_{0})|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\xi=\lVert T\mathscr{F}^{-1}(x\varphi_{\lambda}(\cdot-\xi_{0}))\rVert_{L^{2}V_{1}}^{2}\leqslant\lVert T\rVert^{2}\lVert x\rVert^{2}_{V_{0}}.

Since
m\in L^{\infty}_{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1}))
, the map
\xi\mapsto\left\lVert m(\xi)x\right\rVert_{V_{1}}^{2}
is locally integrable; thus, by Lebesgue’s differentiation theorem, for each
x\in V_{0}
we obtain a full measure set
E_{x}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\xi_{0}\in E_{x}
implies that
\lVert m(\xi_{0})x\rVert_{V_{1}}\leqslant\lVert T\rVert\lVert x\rVert_{V_{0}}
. Since
V_{0}
is separable, we can let
\{{x_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a dense subset of
V_{0}
and set
E=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}E_{x_{n}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. It follows that
E
has full measure and
\xi_{0}\in E
implies that
\lVert m(\xi_{0})\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(V_{0},V_{1})}\leqslant\lVert T\rVert
. Thus,
\lVert m\rVert\leqslant\lVert T\rVert
, and the proof of (
3.6
) is complete.

We now turn to the construction of the multiplier
m
from the map
T
, assuming it commutes with translations. Since
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
are separable Hilbert spaces, we can find sequences
\{{\Pi^{i}_{N}}\}_{N=0}^{\infty}
of orthogonal projection operators on
V_{i}
such that for
i\in\{{0,1}\}
we have that
\mathrm{dim}(\Pi^{i}_{N}V_{i})=N
and
\Pi^{i}_{N}V_{i}\subset\Pi^{i}_{N+1}V_{i}
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\lim_{N\to\infty}\Pi_{N}^{i}x=
for all
x\in V_{i}
.

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define the maps
S_{N}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\Pi^{0}_{N}V_{0})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\Pi^{1}_{N}V_{1})
via
S_{N}f=\Pi^{1}_{N}Tf
. It is a simple matter to check that each
S_{N}
commutes with translations. Since
\Pi^{0}_{N}V_{0}
and
\Pi^{1}_{N}V_{1}
are finite dimensional, Theorem
3.1
then provides
\mu_{N}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{L}(\Pi^{0}_{N}V_{0};\Pi^{1}_{N}V_{1}))
such that
S_{N}=\mu_{N}(D)
and
\left\lVert S_{N}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}}=\left\lVert\mu_{N}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}
. We then define the symbols
\{{m_{N}}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{L}(V_{0},V_{1}))
via
m_{N}=\mu_{N}\Pi^{0}_{N}
, which means that

(3.8)

m_{N}(D)=S_{N}\Pi^{0}_{N}=\Pi^{1}_{N}T\Pi^{0}_{N},\hskip 10.00002pt\lVert m_{N}\rVert_{L^{\infty}\mathcal{L}(V_{0},V_{1})}\leqslant\lVert T\rVert,\text{ and }\Pi^{1}_{N}m_{N+1}\Pi^{0}_{N}=m_{N}.

Note that we are free to modify each symbol in the sequence on a set of measure zero and obtain the pointwise inequality
\lVert m_{N}(\xi)\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(V_{0},V_{1})}\leqslant\lVert T\rVert
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Given
\xi_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
x\in V_{0}
,
y\in V_{1}
, and
N,M\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that

(3.9)

\langle y,(m_{N+M}(\xi_{0})-m_{N}(\xi_{0}))x\rangle=\langle y,m_{N+M}(\xi_{0})(1-\Pi^{0}_{N})x\rangle+\langle y,(1-\Pi_{N}^{1})m_{N+M}(\xi_{0})\Pi_{N}^{0}x\rangle,

and hence

(3.10)

\limsup_{N,M\to\infty}\char 12\relax{\langle y,(m_{N+M}(\xi_{0})-m_{N}(\xi_{0}))x\rangle}\leqslant\lim_{N\to\infty}\lVert T\rVert(\lVert y\rVert_{V_{1}}\lVert(1-\Pi_{N}^{0})x\rVert_{V_{0}}+\lVert(1-\Pi_{N})y\rVert_{V_{1}}\lVert x\rVert_{V_{0}})=0.

Thus,
\{{\langle y,m_{N}(\xi_{0})x\rangle}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{L}(V_{0},V_{1})
is Cauchy, and hence convergent. Using this and the established bounds on
m_{N}
together with Theorem VI.1 of Reed and Simon
[
74
]
, we acquire
m(\xi_{0})\in\mathcal{L}(V_{0},V_{1})
such that
\lVert m(\xi_{0})\rVert\leqslant\lVert T\rVert
and
\langle y,m_{N}(\xi_{0})x\rangle\to\langle y,m(\xi_{0})x\rangle
as
N\to\infty
for all
x\in V_{0}
and
y\in V_{1}
. It then follows from Theorem
3.3
that
\xi_{0}\mapsto m(\xi_{0})
is strongly measurable, since
\langle y,mx\rangle
is the pointwise limit of measurable functions for every
y\in V_{1}
and
x\in V_{0}
. Synthesizing this information, we find that
m\in L^{\infty}_{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1})).

To complete the proof, it only remains to check that
m(D)=T
. For this, we use Parseval’s theorem for fixed
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{0})
and
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{1})
to write

(3.11)

\langle g,Tf\rangle=\int_{\Omega}\langle\mathscr{F}[g](\xi),m_{N}(\xi)\mathscr{F}[f](\xi)\rangle\;\mathrm{d}\xi+\langle(1-\Pi_{N}^{1})g,T\Pi_{N}^{0}f\rangle+\langle g,T(1-\Pi_{N}^{0})f\rangle.

We then apply the dominated convergence theorem while sending
N\to\infty
to deduce that
\langle g,Tf\rangle=\langle\mathscr{F}[g],m\mathscr{F}[f]\rangle
. Hence,
m(D)=T
.
∎

The following generalization will also be of use to us. In fact, this corollary is the main workhorse of Section
4
in that it is the dictionary that allows us to transfer operator bounds derived from solving PDEs to bounds on the derivatives of the symbol of a special vector-valued Fourier multiplication operator.

Corollary 3.6

(Translation commuting linear maps, infinite dimensional case II)
.

Suppose that
W,V_{0},V_{1}
are separable Hilbert spaces over
\mathbb{C}
with
V_{1}\hookrightarrow V_{0}
, and suppose that for some
s_{0},s_{1},s\in\mathbb{R}
we have a translation commuting and continuous linear map

(3.12)

T:H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{0})\cap H^{s_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{1})\to H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};W).

Then, there exists a unique (up to modification on sets of measure zero) locally essentially bounded and strongly measurable (in the sense of the second item of Definition
3.2
) function
m:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{L}(V_{1};W)
such that
T=m(D)
; moreover,
m
obeys the estimate

(3.13)

\langle\xi\rangle^{s}\lVert m(\xi)x\rVert_{W}\lesssim\lVert T\rVert(\langle\xi\rangle^{s_{0}}\lVert x\rVert_{V_{0}}+\langle\xi\rangle^{s_{1}}\lVert x\rVert_{V_{1}}),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\;x\in V_{1}

for almost every
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The above implicit constant only depends on
d
,
s
,
s_{0}
, and
s_{1}
.

Proof.

For
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
we let

(3.14)

A_{\ell}=\begin{cases}B(0,1)&\text{if }\ell=0,\\
B(0,2^{\ell})\setminus B(0,2^{\ell-1})&\text{if }\ell\geqslant 1,\end{cases}

T_{\ell}=T\mathds{1}_{A_{\ell}}(D)
, and

(3.15)

\lVert y\rVert_{W^{(\ell)}}=\langle 2^{\ell}\rangle^{s}\lVert y\rVert_{W},\hskip 10.00002pt\lVert x\rVert_{V_{1}^{(\ell)}}=\sqrt{\langle 2^{\ell}\rangle^{2s_{0}}\lVert x\rVert_{V_{0}}^{2}+\langle 2^{\ell}\rangle^{2s_{1}}\lVert x\rVert_{V_{1}}^{2}}

for
y\in W
and
x\in V_{1}
. We consider
W
equipped with the norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{W^{(\ell)}}
, denoted
W^{(\ell)}
, and
V_{1}
equipped with the norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{V_{1}^{(\ell)}}
, similarly denoted
V_{1}^{(\ell)}
, and apply Theorem (
3.5
) to
T_{\ell}
, viewed as a map
T_{\ell}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};W^{(\ell)}_{1})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};W^{(\ell)})
. The hypothesis (
3.12
) and the usual Fourier characterization of
H^{r}
Sobolev norms then provide the estimate

(3.16)

\lVert T_{\ell}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}V_{1}^{(\ell)};L^{2}W^{(\ell)})}\lesssim\lVert T\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(H^{s_{0}}V_{0}\cap H^{s_{1}}V_{1};H^{s}W)},

where the implicit constant depends on
d
,
s
,
s_{0}
, and
s_{1}
but not on
\ell
.
Then the associated multiplier
m_{\ell}\in L_{\ast}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{L}(V_{1}^{(\ell)},W^{(\ell)}))
granted from Theorem
3.5
obeys the bounds

(3.17)

\langle 2^{\ell}\rangle^{s}\lVert m_{\ell}(\xi)x\rVert_{W}\lesssim\lVert T\rVert(\langle 2^{\ell}\rangle^{s_{0}}\lVert x\rVert_{H^{s_{0}}}+\langle 2^{\ell}\rangle^{s_{1}}\lVert x\rVert_{V_{1}})

for every
x\in V_{1}
and almost every
\xi\in A_{\ell}
. Note that
m_{\ell}
can be modified on a set of measure zero and made to have support contained in
A_{\ell}
. To conclude, we take
m=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}m_{\ell}
. It is then straightforward to check that
m(D)=T
and, by using (
3.17
), that estimate (
3.13
) holds.
∎

3.2.
Classical results in vector-valued Harmonic analysis

We now turn our attention to a collection of classical results in vector-valued harmonic analysis. We showcase these here for two reasons. First, in the subsequent subsection we will develop some variants and generalizations that will play a crucial role in our study of the linear PDEs (
2.1
) and (
2.2
). Second, we will need them in our development of some Sobolev-type function spaces in Section
5.1
.

We begin by recording a pair of well-known multiplier theorems. The first up is the scalar-valued Marcinkiewicz theorem, for which a proof can be found in Corollary 6.25 of Grafakos
[
39
]
.

Theorem 3.7

(Marcinkiewicz)
.

Let
m:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
be a bounded function that is
d
-times continuously differentiable away from the coordinate axes in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Assume that there exists a constant
A\geqslant 0
such that for all
k\in\{{1,\dots,d}\}
, each choice of distinct
j_{1},\dots,j_{k}\in\{{1,\dots,d}\}
, and every
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\xi_{r}\neq 0
for
r\notin\{{j_{1},\dotsc,j_{k}}\}
we have that

(3.18)

|(\partial_{j_{1}}\cdots\partial_{j_{k}}m)(\xi)|\leqslant A|\xi_{j_{1}}|^{-1}\cdots|\xi_{j_{k}}|^{-1}.

Then the map
m(D)=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
uniquely extends to a bounded linear map
m(D):L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
for every
1<p<\infty
, and

(3.19)

\left\lVert m(D)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p})}\leqslant C_{p,d}(A+\left\lVert m\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}})

for a constant
C_{p,d}>0
depending only on
d
and
p
. If, in addition, we have that
m(-\xi)=\overline{m(\xi)}
for a.e.
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then
m
is reality preserving in the sense that
m(D):L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
.

We next record a vector-valued version of the celebrated Mikhlin-Hörmander multiplier theorem, originally due to Schwartz
[
76
]
. For a proof of the following formulation we refer to Proposition 6.16 in Bergh and Löfström
[
23
]
.

Theorem 3.8

(Mikhlin-Hörmander)
.

Let
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
be two separable complex Hilbert spaces and let
\mathbb{N}\ni\ell>d/2
. Suppose that
m\in C^{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{{0}\};\mathcal{L}(V_{0},V_{1}))
satisfies

(3.20)

\max_{|\alpha|\leqslant\ell}\sup_{\xi\neq 0}|\xi|^{|\alpha|}\lVert\partial^{\alpha}m(\xi)\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(H_{0},H_{1})}\leqslant C_{\ell}

for a constant
C_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Then the map
m(D):L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{0})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{1})
uniquely extends to a bounded linear map
m(D):L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{0})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{1})
, and

(3.21)

\lVert m(D)\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}V_{0},L^{p}V_{1})}\lesssim C_{\ell},

where the implicit constant depends only on
d
and
p
.

We now record an important maximal inequality due to Fefferman and Stein
[
34
]
(see also Theorem 1 in Chapter 2 of Stein
[
88
]
). In what follows
\mathcal{M}
denotes the usual Hardy-Littlewood maximal function.

Theorem 3.9

(Fefferman-Stein maximal inequality)
.

Suppose that
\{{f_{\ell}}\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\subseteq L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then for
1<p<\infty
we have the inequality

(3.22)

\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}|\mathcal{M}(f_{\ell})|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}\lesssim\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}|f_{\ell}|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}.

Next, we record some vector-valued adaptations of well-known estimates from Littlewood-Paley theory. The first is based on Theorem 6.1.2 and Proposition 6.1.4 of Grafakos
[
39
]
.

Theorem 3.10

(Annular Littlewood-Paley, I)
.

Let
V
be a separable complex Hilbert space, and let
s\in[0,\infty)
,
1<p<\infty
, and
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose that
\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
are such that for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
we have that
\mathscr{F}[\varphi_{j}]
is supported in the annulus
B(0,2^{m+j})\setminus\overline{B(0,2^{-m+j})}
and there exists a constants
\{{C_{\alpha}}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}
such that

(3.23)

|\partial^{\alpha}\varphi_{j}(\xi)|\leqslant C_{\alpha}2^{-j|\alpha|}\text{ for all }\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ and }\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}.

Then for every
f\in H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};E)
we have the inequality

(3.24)

\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2s}\lVert\varphi_{j}(D)f\rVert_{V}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s,p}V},

with implicit constants depending only on
\varphi
,
s
,
p
,
d
,
m
, and finitely many of the
\{{C_{\alpha}}\}_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that for
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
we have the inequality

(3.25)

\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2s}\lVert\varphi_{j}(D)\langle D\rangle^{-s}f\rVert_{V}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}V}.

But this is a direct application of the Mikhlin-Hörmander multiplier theorem, Theorem
3.8
, with the smooth multiplier
m:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{L}(V;\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z};V))
given by

(3.26)

m(\xi)x=\{{\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{s}\varphi_{j}(\xi)x/\langle\xi\rangle^{s}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\text{ for }\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ and }x\in V.

∎

Our next two results are loosely based on Lemmas 2.1.F and 2.1.G from Taylor
[
93
]
.

Theorem 3.11

(Annular Littlewood-Paley, II)
.

Let
V
be a separable complex Hilbert space, and fix
s\in[0,\infty)
,
1<p<\infty
, and
m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
. For
j\in\mathbb{Z}
let
A_{j}=B(0,2^{j+m})\setminus\overline{B(0,2^{j-m})}
. Suppose that
\{{f_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\subset L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
satisfy
\supp\mathscr{F}[f_{j}]\subset A_{j}
for every
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then

(3.27)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\subset\mathbb{Z}\\
F\text{ finite}\end{subarray}}\Big\lVert\sum_{j\in F}f_{j}\Big\rVert_{H^{s,p}V}\lesssim\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2s}\lVert f_{j}\rVert_{V}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}},

with the implicit constant depending only on
d
,
s
,
p
, and
m
. Moreover, if the right hand side is finite, then the series
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{j}
converges unconditionally in
H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
.

Proof.

Let
1<q<\infty
satisfy
1/p+1/q=1
, and let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(A_{-1}\cup A_{0}\cup A_{1})
be such that
\varphi=1
on
\overline{A_{0}}
. Given
g\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
and a finite set
F\subset\mathbb{Z}
, we compute

(3.28)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big\langle\langle D\rangle^{s}\sum_{j\in F}f_{j},g\Big\rangle=\sum_{j\in F}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big\langle\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{s}f_{j},\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(D)g\big\rangle

where
\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(\xi)=\langle\xi\rangle^{s}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{-s}\varphi(\xi/2^{j})
. Hence, we can apply Cauchy-Schwartz, Hölder, Theorem
3.10
, and duality (See, e.g., Theorem 5 of Section 4 in Chapter 12 of Dinculeanu
[
31
]
) to acquire the bound

(3.29)

\Big\lVert\sum_{j\in F}f_{j}\Big\rVert_{H^{s,p}V}=\sup_{\lVert g\rVert_{L^{q}V}\leqslant 1}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big\langle\langle D\rangle^{s}\sum_{j\in F}f_{j},g\Big\rangle\Big|\lesssim\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2s}\lVert f_{j}\rVert_{V}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}.

Estimate (
3.27
) follows. A similar strategy shows that if the right hand side of (
3.27
) is finite, and
\{F_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is any increasing sequence of finite subsets of
\mathbb{Z}
such that
\mathbb{Z}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F_{n}
, then
\{\sum_{j\in F_{n}}f_{j}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy (and hence convergent) in
H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
, and the limit is independent of the choice of the sequence. This implies the unconditional convergence of the series. We omit further details for the sake of brevity.
∎

Finally, we record a non-annular Littlewood-Paley estimate. Note that in this result it is important that we are considering spaces of positive regularity.

Theorem 3.12

(Ball Littlewood-Paley)
.

Let
V
be a separable complex Hilbert space, and fix
s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
1<p<\infty
, and
m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
. For
j\in\mathbb{N}
set
B_{j}=B(0,2^{j+m})
. Suppose that
\{{f_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
satisfy
\supp\mathscr{F}[f_{j}]\subset B_{j}
for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

(3.30)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\subset\mathbb{N}\\
F\text{ finite}\end{subarray}}\Big\lVert\sum_{j\in F}f_{j}\Big\rVert_{H^{s,p}V}\lesssim\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}4^{js}\lVert f_{j}\rVert_{V}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}},

with the implicit constant depending only on
d
,
s
,
p
, and
m
. Moreover, if the right hand side is finite, then the series
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}f_{j}
converges unconditionally in
H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
.

Proof.

Let
\{{\varphi_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
be an inhomogeneous Littlewood-Paley partition of unity with
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\varphi_{k}=1
,
\varphi_{k}=\varphi_{1}(\cdot/2^{k})
for
k\geqslant 1
,
\supp\varphi_{0}\subseteq B(0,2)
,
\supp\varphi_{k}\subseteq B(0,2^{k+2})\setminus\overline{B(0,2^{k-2})}
. We also write
\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}=2^{-ks}\langle\cdot\rangle^{s}\varphi_{k}
.

Let
1<q<\infty
satisfy
1/p+1/q=1
, and let
g\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
. Then for any finite
F\subset\mathbb{N}
we have that

(3.31)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big\langle\langle D\rangle^{s}\sum_{j\in F}f_{j},g\Big\rangle=\sum_{j\in F}\sum_{k=0}^{j+3+m}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle\langle D\rangle^{s}f_{j},\varphi_{k}(D)g\rangle=\sum_{j\in F}\sum_{k=0}^{j+3+m}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle 2^{js}f_{j},2^{(k-j)s}\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}(D)g\rangle.

Hence, we may use Cauchy-Schwarz, Young’s convolution inequality, and Hölder’s inequality to bound

(3.32)

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big\langle\langle D\rangle^{s}\sum_{j\in F}f_{j},g\Big\rangle\Big|\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(\sum_{j\in F}4^{js}\lVert f_{j}\rVert_{V}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\sum_{j\in F}\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{j+3+m}2^{s(k-j)}\lVert\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}(D)g\rVert_{V}\Big)^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\\
\lesssim\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}4^{js}\lVert f_{j}\rVert_{V}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\lVert\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}(D)g\rVert_{V}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{q}}.

Theorem
3.10
provides the bound

(3.33)

\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\lVert\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}(D)g\rVert_{V}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{q}}\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{L^{q}V}.

Taking the supremum over
g
such that
\lVert g\rVert_{L^{q}V}\leqslant 1
in (
3.32
) then gives the desired estimate. The unconditional convergence of the series then follows from a variant of this bound as in the proof of Theorem
3.11
.
∎

3.3.
On a novel variant of the Mikhlin-Hörmander multiplier theorem

We now return to the topic of multipliers, with the aim of deriving a generalized version of Mikhlin-Hörmander (the classical vector-valued version is Theorem
3.8
). The task of generalizing Mikhlin-Hörmander is, of course, not new, and there are many works in the existing literature that do so. We pause here briefly to review these. In unpublished work, Pisier showed that if a vector-valued Mikhlin-Hörmander theorem holds for an
\mathcal{L}(E)
-valued symbol, then
E
is isomorphic to a Hilbert space. Note that Lancien, Lancien, and Le Merdy
[
54
]
provide a proof of Pisier’s unpublished result in Remark 6.4, as a consequence of another result. The takeaway is that there is an obstruction to versions of Mikhlin-Hörmander for maps valued in
\mathcal{L}(B_{0};B_{1})
if the
B_{i}
spaces are only Banach. The work of Bourgain
[
25
]
, Burkholder
[
27
]
, McConnell
[
58
]
, Zimmermann
[
97
]
found a workaround for scalar-valued symbol multipliers if the Banach space is an unconditional martingale difference (UMD) space and the Mikhlin-Hörmander hypotheses are strengthened a bit. However, it is known that UMD spaces are reflexive. The scalar UMD extension was later strengthened to operator-valued symbols by Amann
[
13
]
, Hieber
[
47
]
, Haller, Heck, and Noll
[
45
]
, and Giardi and Weis
[
37
]
. We also refer also to Chapter 4 of Prüss and Simonett
[
72
]
and the survey of Giardi and Weis
[
38
]
for more information.

The Banach obstruction can also be overcome by changing from
L^{p}
spaces to others. Amann
[
13
]
developed a version of Mikhlin-Hörmander in the context of vector-valued Besov spaces, which was subsequently extended by Giardi and Weis
[
36
]
. The setting of Triebel-Lizorkin spaces was considered by Bu and Kim
[
26
]
.

For our purposes in this paper, we can restrict to multipliers that take values in
\mathcal{L}(V;W)
, where
V
and
W
are separable Hilbert spaces. However, we need a version of Mikhlin-Hörmander that allows us to replace a single
L^{p}
space with the more general setting of
H^{s_{0},p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{0})\cap H^{s_{1},p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{1})
, the intersection of different Bessel potential spaces with values in different Hilbert spaces such that
V_{1}\hookrightarrow V_{0}
. We will prove this new variant by combining three main ingredients. The first is the annular Littlewood-Paley results of Theorems
3.10
and
3.11
, while the second is the Fefferman-Stein maximal inequality of Theorem
3.9
. The final ingredient we need, which we record in the next result, gives pointwise bounds via maximal functions. It is a generalization of Lemma 2.2 in Bahouri, Chemin, and Danchin
[
15
]
.

Theorem 3.13

(Pointwise bounds for spectrally localized Fourier multipliers)
.

Suppose
V
,
V_{0}
, and
V_{1}
are separable complex Hilbert spaces such that
V_{1}\hookrightarrow V_{0}
. Let
s,s_{0},s_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mu,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\mu\leqslant 0
and
d<\ell
. Suppose that
m\in C^{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{{0}\};\mathcal{L}(V_{1},V))
satisfies

(3.34)

\langle\xi\rangle^{s}\max_{|\alpha|\leqslant\ell}|\xi|^{|\alpha|}\lVert\partial^{\alpha}m(\xi)x\rVert_{V}\leqslant C_{\ell}|\xi|^{\mu}(\langle\xi\rangle^{s_{0}}\lVert x\rVert_{V_{0}}+\langle\xi\rangle^{s_{1}}\lVert x\rVert_{V_{1}})

for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{{0}\}
and
x\in V_{1}
. Let
\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B(0,16)\setminus\overline{B(0,1/16)})
be such that
\widetilde{\varphi}=1
on the support of
\varphi
. Then for
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{1})
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have the pointwise estimate

(3.35)

\langle\lambda\rangle^{s}\lVert m(D)\varphi(D/\lambda)f(z)\rVert_{V}\lesssim C_{\ell}|\lambda|^{\mu}\left[\langle\lambda\rangle^{s_{0}}\mathcal{M}(\lVert\widetilde{\varphi}(D/\lambda)f\rVert_{V_{0}})(z)+\langle\lambda\rangle^{s_{1}}\mathcal{M}(\lVert\widetilde{\varphi}(D/\lambda)f\rVert_{V_{1}})(z)\right].

Proof.

We let
K_{\lambda}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{L}(V_{1};V)
be defined via

(3.36)

K_{\lambda}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}z\cdot\xi}\varphi(\xi)m(\lambda\xi)\;\mathrm{d}\xi,

and note that

(3.37)

m(D)\varphi(D/\lambda)f=\lambda^{d}K_{\lambda}(\lambda(\cdot))\ast(\widetilde{\varphi}(D/\lambda)f)

for all
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{1})
.

Fix
x\in V_{1}
. Hypothesis (
3.34
) implies the rescaled form

(3.38)

\lVert\partial^{\beta}(m(\lambda\cdot))(\xi)x\rVert_{V}\leqslant C_{\ell}|\lambda|^{\mu}\langle\lambda\xi\rangle^{-s}|\xi|^{\mu-|\beta|}(\langle\lambda\xi\rangle^{s_{0}}\lVert x\rVert_{V_{0}}+\langle\lambda\xi\rangle^{s_{1}}\lVert x\rVert_{V_{1}})

for every
|\beta|\leqslant\ell
. This, integration by parts, and an application of the Leibniz rule then provide the following kernel bound for
|\alpha|\leqslant\ell
:

(3.39)

\lVert(2\pi\mathrm{i}z)^{\alpha}K_{\lambda}(z)x\rVert_{V}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert\partial^{\alpha}(\varphi m(\lambda(\cdot)))(\xi)x\rVert^{2}_{V}\;\mathrm{d}\xi\lesssim C_{\ell}\langle\lambda\rangle^{-s}|\lambda|^{\mu}(\langle\lambda\rangle^{s_{0}}\lVert x\rVert_{V_{0}}+\langle\lambda\rangle^{s_{1}}\lVert x\rVert_{V_{1}}).

Summing over
|\alpha|\leqslant\ell
, we deduce from this that

(3.40)

\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle z\rangle^{\ell}\lVert K_{\lambda}(z)x\rVert_{V}\lesssim C_{\ell}\langle\lambda\rangle^{-s}|\lambda|^{\mu}(\langle\lambda\rangle^{s_{0}}\lVert x\rVert_{V_{0}}+\langle\lambda\rangle^{s_{1}}\lVert x\rVert_{V_{1}}).

Now we return to formula (
3.37
) to obtain the claimed pointwise bounds. Let
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{1})
, and set
g=\widetilde{\varphi}(D/\lambda)f
. Write

(3.41)

A_{j}(y,\lambda)=\begin{cases}B(y,1/\lambda)&\text{if }j=0,\\
B(y,2^{j}/\lambda)\setminus B(y,2^{j-1}/\lambda)&\text{if }j\geqslant 1.\end{cases}

Then the above estimates allow us to bound

(3.42)

\lVert(\lambda^{d}K_{\lambda}(\lambda\cdot)\ast g)(y)\rVert_{V}\leqslant\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\int_{A_{j}(y,\lambda)}\lambda^{d}\lVert K_{\lambda}(\lambda(y-z))g(z)\rVert_{V}\;\mathrm{d}z\\
\lesssim C_{\ell}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\int_{A_{j}(y,\lambda)}\frac{\lambda^{d}}{\langle\lambda(y-z)\rangle^{\ell}}\langle\lambda\rangle^{-s}|\lambda|^{\mu}(\langle\lambda\rangle^{s_{0}}\lVert g(z)\rVert_{V_{0}}+\langle\lambda\rangle^{s_{1}}\lVert g(z)\rVert_{V_{1}})\;\mathrm{d}z\\
\lesssim C_{\ell}\langle\lambda\rangle^{-s}|\lambda|^{\mu}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{d-\ell}\Big(\frac{1}{|B(y,2^{j}/\lambda)|}\int_{B(y,2^{j}/\lambda)}(\langle\lambda\rangle^{s_{0}}\lVert g(z)\rVert_{V_{0}}+\langle\lambda\rangle^{s_{1}}\lVert g(z)\rVert_{V_{1}})\;\mathrm{d}z\Big)\\
\lesssim C_{\ell}\langle\lambda\rangle^{-s}|\lambda|^{\mu}\left[\langle\lambda\rangle^{s_{0}}\mathcal{M}(\lVert g\rVert_{V_{0}})(y)+\langle\lambda\rangle^{s_{1}}\mathcal{M}(\lVert g\rVert_{V_{1}})(y)\right].

This yields the desired bound upon substituting in
g=\widetilde{\varphi}(D/\lambda)f
.
∎

We now have all the tools needed to prove our generalization of the Mikhlin-Hörmander multiplier theorem.

Theorem 3.14

(Mikhlin-Hörmander, novel form)
.

Suppose
V
,
V_{0}
, and
V_{1}
are separable complex Hilbert spaces such that
V_{1}\hookrightarrow V_{0}
. Let
s,s_{0},s_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mu,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\mu\leqslant 0
and
d<\ell
. Suppose that
m\in C^{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{{0}\};\mathcal{L}(V_{1};V))
satisfies

(3.43)

\langle\xi\rangle^{s}\max_{|\beta|\leqslant\ell}|\xi|^{|\beta|}\lVert\partial^{\beta}m(\xi)x\rVert_{V}\leqslant C_{\ell}|\xi|^{\mu}(\langle\xi\rangle^{s_{0}}\lVert x\rVert_{V_{0}}+\langle\xi\rangle^{s_{1}}\lVert x\rVert_{V_{1}})

for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{{0}\}
and
x\in V_{1}
. Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
satisfy
|\alpha|=-\mu
. Then the bounded linear map

(3.44)

m(D)D^{\alpha}:H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{0})\cap H^{s_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{1})\to H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V),

which is well-defined and bounded in light of the
\beta=0
estimate from (
3.43
), uniquely extends to a bounded linear map

(3.45)

m(D)D^{\alpha}:H^{s_{0},p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{0})\cap H^{s_{1},p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{1})\to H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)

for every
1<p<\infty
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi},\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be such that
\supp\varphi\subset B(0,8)\setminus\overline{B(0,1/8)}
,
\supp\widetilde{\varphi},\supp\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}\subset B(0,16)\setminus\overline{B(0,1/16)}
,
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi(\cdot/2^{j})=\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{{0}\}}
,
\widetilde{\varphi}=1
on
\supp\varphi
, and
\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}=1
on
\supp\widetilde{\varphi}
.

Suppose that
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{1})
. We first use the Littlewood-Paley estimate of Theorem
3.11
to bound

(3.46)

\lVert m(D)D^{\alpha}f\rVert_{H^{s,p}V}\lesssim\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2s}\lVert m(D)D^{\alpha}\varphi(D/2^{j})f\rVert^{2}_{V}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}.

Next, for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
we apply Theorem
3.13
with
\lambda=2^{j}
to see that

(3.47)

\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{s}\lVert m(D)D^{\alpha}\varphi(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{V}\lesssim C_{\ell}2^{-j|\alpha|}(\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{s_{0}}\mathcal{M}(\lVert D^{\alpha}\widetilde{\varphi}(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{V_{0}})+\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{s_{1}}\mathcal{M}(\lVert D^{\alpha}\widetilde{\varphi}(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{V_{1}})).

For
i\in\{{0,1}\}
, we may apply Theorem
3.13
again (using the trivial multiplier
m=1
and parameters
s=s_{0}=s_{1}=0
with
V_{i}
used for all three Hilbert spaces), to acquire the bound

(3.48)

\lVert D^{\alpha}\widetilde{\varphi}(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{V_{i}}\lesssim 2^{j|\alpha|}\mathcal{M}(\lVert\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{V_{i}}).

We then combine the estimates (
3.47
) and (
3.48
):

(3.49)

\frac{1}{C_{\ell}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{s}\lVert m(D)D^{\alpha}\varphi(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{V}\lesssim\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{s_{0}}\mathcal{M}\mathcal{M}(\lVert\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{V_{0}})+\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{s_{1}}\mathcal{M}\mathcal{M}(\lVert\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{V_{1}}).

From this, estimate (
3.46
), two applications of the Fefferman-Stein maximal inequality of Theorem
3.9
, and an application of the Littlewood-Paley estimate of Theorem
3.10
we deduce that

(3.50)

\frac{1}{C_{\ell}}\lVert m(D)D^{\alpha}f\rVert_{H^{s,p}V}\\
\lesssim\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2s_{0}}[\mathcal{M}\mathcal{M}(\lVert\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{V_{0}})]^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}+\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2s_{1}}[\mathcal{M}\mathcal{M}(\lVert\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{V_{1}})]^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}\\
\lesssim\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2s_{0}}\lVert\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{V_{0}}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}+\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2s_{1}}\lVert\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{V_{1}}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}\\
\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s_{0},p}V_{0}}+\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s_{1},p}V_{1}}.

This estimate then allows us to extend
m(D)D^{\alpha}
as stated.
∎

4.
Vector-valued symbol calculus for the solution map

The goal of this section is to prove that the solution operator associated to the linear system (
2.2
) is given by an operator-valued Fourier multiplier. Once this is established, we then show that the symbol of the operator is a smooth function of frequency away from zero and satisfies bounds of the type appearing in the hypotheses of the Mikhlin-Hörmander multiplier theorem (see Theorems
3.8
and
3.14
).

4.1.
Preliminaries

The following definition enumerates the translation commuting maps we are interested in extending to an
L^{p}
theory. That the following is well-defined is a consequence of Theorem
2.6
.

Definition 4.1

(Spaces and translation commuting maps)
.

We set the following notation.

(1)

For
s\in\mathbb{N}
we define the spaces

(4.1)

\mathbb{X}_{s}=H^{1+s}(\Omega;\mathbb{C})\times H^{2+s}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})\times H^{3/2+s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{2}),

(4.2)

\mathbb{Y}_{s}=H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})\times H^{1/2+s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{3})\times H^{5/2+s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{2}),

(4.3)

\hskip 10.00002pt\widetilde{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}=\Big\{(g,f,k,h,\omega)\text{ as in~\eqref{this space is too long dear liza}}\;:\;\omega,\;h-\int_{0}^{b}g(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C})\Big\}.

(2)

We define the bounded linear map
\Phi:\widetilde{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}\to\mathbb{X}_{s}
via
\Phi(g,f,k,h,\omega)=(p,u,\chi)
, where the latter tuple is the unique solution to (
2.2
) with data
(g,f,k,h,\omega)
provided by Theorem
2.6
. We also define the bounded linear map
\Psi:\mathbb{Y}_{s}\to\mathbb{X}_{s}
via
\Psi(f,k,H)=\Phi(0,f,k,\nabla_{\|}\cdot H,0)
.

It is clear from the existence and uniqueness result of Theorem
2.6
and the fact that the equations (
2.2
) have constant coefficients that the operators
\Phi
and
\Psi
of the previous definition commute with translations of the tangential variables. Thus, we expect that they are given by Fourier multipliers. The following definition gives the class of the vector-valued Fourier multipliers with which we are concerned. As we will show later,
\Psi
has a multiplier belonging to this admissible class.

Definition 4.2

(Admissible class of vector-valued Fourier multipliers)
.

We make the following definitions for
s\in\mathbb{N}
.

(1)

We say that a symbol

(4.4)

m:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathcal{L}(H^{s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}^{3})\times\mathbb{C}^{3}\times\mathbb{C}^{2};H^{1+s}((0,b);\mathbb{C})\times H^{2+s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}^{3})\times\mathbb{C}^{2}).

belongs to the admissible class
\mathfrak{A}(s)
if it is strongly measurable in the sense of the second item of Definition
3.2
, and upon writing
m
in ‘matrix form’

(4.5)

m=\begin{pmatrix}m_{11}&m_{12}&m_{13}\\
m_{21}&m_{22}&m_{23}\\
m_{31}&m_{32}&m_{33}\end{pmatrix},

we have that there exists a constant
C<\infty
and a full measure set
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that for
\xi\in E
:

(a)

m_{11}(\xi)\in\mathcal{L}(H^{s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}^{3});H^{1+s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}))
obeys the bound

(4.6)

\lVert m_{11}(\xi)\phi\rVert_{H^{1+s}}+\langle\xi\rangle^{1+s}\lVert m_{11}(\xi)\phi\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant C(\lVert\phi\rVert_{H^{s}}+\langle\xi\rangle^{s}\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{2}})

for
\phi\in H^{s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}^{3})
;

(b)

m_{12}(\xi)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{3};H^{1+s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}))
obeys the bound

(4.7)

\lVert m_{12}(\xi)\rVert_{H^{1+s}}+\langle\xi\rangle^{1+s}\lVert m_{12}(\xi)\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant C\langle\xi\rangle^{1/2+s};

(c)

m_{13}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2};H^{1+s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}))

obeys the bound

(4.8)

\lVert m_{13}(\xi)\rVert_{H^{1+s}}+\langle\xi\rangle^{1+s}\lVert m_{13}(\xi)\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant C\langle\xi\rangle^{5/2+s};

(d)

m_{21}(\xi)\in\mathcal{L}(H^{s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}^{3}),H^{2+s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}^{3}))
obeys the bound

(4.9)

\lVert m_{21}(\xi)\phi\rVert_{H^{2+s}}+\langle\xi\rangle^{2+s}\lVert m_{21}(\xi)\phi\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant C(\lVert\phi\rVert_{H^{s}}+\langle\xi\rangle^{s}\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{2}})

for
\phi\in H^{s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}^{3})
;

(e)

m_{22}(\xi)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{3};H^{2+s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}^{3}))
obeys the bound

(4.10)

\lVert m_{22}(\xi)\rVert_{H^{2+s}}+\langle\xi\rangle^{2+s}\lVert m_{22}(\xi)\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant C\langle\xi\rangle^{1/2+s};

(f)

m_{23}(\xi)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2};H^{2+s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}^{3}))
obeys the bound

(4.11)

\lVert m_{23}(\xi)\rVert_{H^{2+s}}+\langle\xi\rangle^{2+s}\lVert m_{23}(\xi)\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant C\langle\xi\rangle^{5/2+s};

(g)

m_{31}(\xi)\in\mathcal{L}(H^{s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}^{3});\mathbb{C}^{2})
obeys the bound

(4.12)

\langle\xi\rangle^{3/2+s}|m_{31}(\xi)\phi|\leqslant C(\lVert\phi\rVert_{H^{s}}+\langle\xi\rangle^{s}\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{2}})

for
\phi\in H^{s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}^{3})
;

(h)

m_{32}(\xi)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{2})
obeys the bound

(4.13)

\langle\xi\rangle^{3/2+s}|m_{32}(\xi)|\leqslant C\langle\xi\rangle^{1/2+s};

(i)

m_{33}(\xi)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{2})
obeys the bound

(4.14)

\langle\xi\rangle^{3/2+s}|m_{33}(\xi)|\leqslant C\langle\xi\rangle^{5/2+s}.

(2)

If
m\in\mathfrak{A}(s)
, then we write
\left\llbracket m\right\rrbracket_{s}\in[0,\infty)
to be the infimum over the constants
C
for which the bounds (
4.6
), (
4.7
), (
4.8
), (
4.9
), (
4.10
), (
4.11
), (
4.12
), (
4.13
), and (
4.14
) hold over a full measure set of frequencies. This makes
\mathfrak{A}(s)
into a Banach space.

Before continuing, we remark that the
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{s}
-norm on
\mathfrak{A}(s)
locally controls essential supremum norm.

Lemma 4.3

(Local essentially uniform control)
.

Fix
s\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
R\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
there exists a constant
C_{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, depending only on
s
and
R
, such that for all
m\in\mathfrak{A}(s)
we have the estimate

(4.15)

\lVert\mathds{1}_{B(0,R)}m\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\ast}\mathcal{L}}\leqslant C_{R}\llbracket m\rrbracket_{s},

where here
\mathcal{L}
refers to the space of linear operators on the right hand side of (
4.4
) and
L^{\infty}_{\ast}\mathcal{L}
is the norm from Definition
3.4
.
Moreover, the map
R\mapsto C_{R}
is bounded on bounded sets.

Proof.

An inspection of the
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{s}
norm in Definition
4.2
shows that we can replace the
\xi
-dependent bounds on the right hand sides of (
4.6
)–(
4.14
) by their value at
R
to derive (
4.15
).
∎

The utility of Definition
4.2
is seen in the next result.

Proposition 4.4

(Symbols and translation commuting maps)
.

The following are equivalent for
s\in\mathbb{N}
and a bounded linear map
T:\mathbb{Y}_{s}\to\mathbb{X}_{s}
:

(1)

T
commutes with the collection of tangential translation operators in the sense that for all
Y\in\mathbb{Y}_{s}
and all
h\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfying
h\cdot e_{3}=0
, it holds that
(TY)(\cdot+h)=T(Y(\cdot+h))
.

(2)

There exists
m\in\mathfrak{A}(s)
such that
T=m(D)
.

In either case,
m
is unique up to modification on a set of measure zero, and we have that

(4.16)

\llbracket m\rrbracket_{s}\asymp\lVert T\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{Y}_{s};\mathbb{X}_{s})}

with implicit constants depending only on
s
.

Proof.

Thanks to the norm equivalence of Lemma
5.2
with
p=2
, each of the nine components of the ‘matrix’ of
T
satisfies the hypotheses of Corollary
3.6
. Thus, by enumerating the estimates on each component, we find that we are granted a multiplier
m\in\mathfrak{A}(s)
such that
T=m(D)
. Estimate (
3.13
) implies that
\llbracket m\rrbracket_{s}\lesssim\lVert T\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{Y}_{s};\mathbb{X}_{s})}
. The opposite inequality and the fact that the second item implies the first are immediate from Plancherel’s theorem.
∎

Our next result relates translations of the symbol to conjugation of the operator by complex exponentials. This is the key that allows us to recast questions of smoothness for multipliers in the language of PDE on the spatial side.

Proposition 4.5

(Symbol translation)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
, and suppose that
T:\mathbb{Y}_{s}\to\mathbb{X}_{s}
is a tangentially translation commuting bounded linear map with associated symbol
m\in\mathfrak{A}(s)
. Then for all
Y=(f,k,H)\in\mathbb{Y}_{s}
and all
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
we have the identity

(4.17)

m(D+\zeta)Y=\mathbf{e}_{-\zeta}(T(\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}Y)),

as an equality in the space
\mathbb{X}_{s}
, where
\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}(\xi)=e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\xi\cdot\zeta}
for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
m(D+\zeta)=(m(\cdot+\zeta))(D)
.

Proof.

With
Y
and
\zeta
as in the statement, we have that the second item of Proposition
4.4
applies and we have
T(\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}Y)=m(D)(\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}Y)
. On the other hand, it is clear by properties of the Fourier transform

(4.18)

\mathscr{F}[\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}m(D+\zeta)Y]=\mathscr{F}[m(D+\zeta)Y](\cdot-\zeta)=m\mathscr{F}[Y](\cdot-\zeta)=m\mathscr{F}[\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}Y].

By taking inverse Fourier transforms, we reveal identity (
4.17
).
∎

4.2.
Derivative estimates for the symbol

The main symbols of concern in this subsection are given by the following definition. The notation here is from Definitions
4.1
and
4.2
.

Definition 4.6

(Main symbol)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
. We denote by
\mathbf{m}\in\mathfrak{A}(s)
the symbol associated with the translation commuting bounded linear map
\Psi:\mathbb{Y}_{s}\to\mathbb{X}_{s}
whose existence is guaranteed by Proposition
4.4
.

Our aim now is to study the differentiability of
\mathbf{m}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
. In doing so, it will be very convenient to introduce some minor abuse of notation in order to make various expressions easier to read. This abuse, the use of which we confine to this subsection, is to view any vector
\theta\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
as being contained in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
via
(\theta,0)\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
. For example, this shorthand means that for
v\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
and
\theta\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
,

(4.19)

\theta\cdot v=(\theta,0)\cdot v=\sum_{j=1}^{2}\theta_{j}v_{j},

and so on. This abuse will only be used in equations that are naturally understood to be posed in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
.

Our next result explores the manifestations of symbol translation on the PDE side.

Proposition 4.7

(Spatial realization of symbol translation)
.

Suppose that
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
(f,k,H)\in\mathbb{Y}_{s}
, and
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then
\mathbf{m}(D+\zeta)(f,k,H)=(p_{\zeta},u_{\zeta},\chi_{\zeta})\in\mathbb{X}_{s}

obeys the following equations

(4.20)

\begin{cases}\mathfrak{g}\chi_{\zeta}+\nabla p_{\zeta}+2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta p_{\zeta}-\mu\nabla\cdot\mathbb{D}u_{\zeta}-\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\nabla(\zeta\cdot u_{\zeta})-\mu 4\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\nabla u_{\zeta}+\mu 4\pi^{2}|\zeta|^{2}u_{\zeta}=f&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\nabla\cdot u_{\zeta}+2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot u_{\zeta}=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\
-(p_{\zeta}I-\mu\mathbb{D}u_{\zeta})e_{3}+\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta(u_{\zeta}\cdot e_{3})-\kappa\nabla_{\|}\cdot\chi_{\zeta}e_{3}-\kappa 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\chi_{\zeta}e_{3}=k&\text{on }\Sigma,\\
\nabla_{\|}^{\perp}\cdot\chi_{\zeta}+2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta^{\perp}\cdot\chi_{\zeta}=0&\text{on }\Sigma,\\
u_{\zeta}\cdot e_{3}=\nabla_{\|}\cdot H+2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot H&\text{on }\Sigma,\\
u_{\zeta}=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

Proof.

We invoke Proposition
4.5
and Definition
4.6
to see that
(p_{\zeta},u_{\zeta},\chi_{\zeta})=\mathbf{e}_{-\zeta}\Psi(\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}f,\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}k,\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}H)
, and hence
(\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}p_{\zeta},\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}u_{\zeta},\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}\chi_{\zeta})
is a solution to the equations (
2.2
) with data
(0,\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}f,\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}k,\nabla_{\|}\cdot(\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}H),0)
. We can then derive equations for
(p_{\zeta},u_{\zeta},\chi_{\zeta})
by expanding with the Leibniz rule and then multiplying by
\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}
. This results in the system (
4.20
).
∎

The following lemma gives a simple estimate for the solution to (
4.20
) and, more importantly, a relationship between the translated symbol and the operators
\Phi
and
\Psi
from Definition
4.1
.

Lemma 4.8

(Estimate and identity for the translated symbol PDE)
.

The following hold for
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
(f,k,H)\in\mathbb{Y}_{s}
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
(p_{\zeta},u_{\zeta},\chi_{\zeta})=\mathbf{m}(D+\zeta)(f,k,H)
.

(1)

We have the estimate

(4.21)

\lVert p_{\zeta},u_{\zeta},\chi_{\zeta}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\leqslant C_{\zeta}\lVert\Psi\rVert_{\mathcal{L}}\lVert f,k,H\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}},

where the constant
\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni\zeta\mapsto C_{\zeta}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
is bounded on bounded sets.

(2)

If
0\not\in\supp\mathscr{F}(f,k,H)
, then

(4.22)

(p_{\zeta},u_{\zeta},\chi_{\zeta})=\Psi(f,k,H)+\Phi\begin{pmatrix}-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot u_{\zeta}\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta p_{\zeta}+\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\nabla(\zeta\cdot u_{\zeta})+\mu 4\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\nabla u_{\zeta}-\mu 4\pi^{2}|\zeta|^{2}u_{\zeta}\\
\kappa 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\chi_{\zeta}e_{3}-\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta(u_{\zeta}\cdot e_{3})\\
2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot H\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta^{\perp}\cdot\chi_{\zeta}\end{pmatrix}.

Proof.

For the first item, we note that
(\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}p_{\zeta},\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}u_{\zeta},\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}\chi_{\zeta})=\Psi(\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}f,\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}k,\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}H)
and hence

(4.23)

\lVert p_{\zeta},u_{\zeta},\chi_{\zeta}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\lesssim_{\zeta}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}p_{\zeta},\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}u_{\zeta},\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}\chi_{\zeta}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\leqslant\lVert\Psi\rVert_{\mathcal{L}}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}f,\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}k,\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}H\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}}\lesssim_{\zeta}\lVert\Psi\rVert_{\mathcal{L}}\lVert f,k,H\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}}.

Next, we use the system (
4.20
) from Proposition
4.7
along with Definition
4.1
to derive the equation (
4.22
). Note that the hypotheses
0\not\in\supp\mathscr{F}(f,k,H)
ensures that the argument of
\Phi
in (
4.22
) belongs to its domain
\widetilde{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}
.
∎

Motivated by formula (
4.22
), we now construct the translation commuting linear maps whose symbols will turn out to be the derivatives of
\mathbf{m}
. In what follows
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}
denotes the symmetric group on the set
\{{1,\dots,\ell}\}
.

Definition 4.9

(Iterative derivative construction)
.

Let
(f,k,H)\in\mathbb{Y}_{s}
satisfy
0\not\in\supp\mathscr{F}(f,k,H)
and set
(p,u,\chi)=\Psi(f,k,H)\in\mathbb{X}_{s}
. For
j\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
we define the
j
-multilinear and symmetric maps

(4.24)

(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{j}\ni(\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j})\mapsto(p^{(j)},u^{(j)},\chi^{(j)})[\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j}]\in\mathbb{X}_{s}

via the following inductive procedure. If
j=1
, we set

(4.25)

(p^{(1)},u^{(1)},\chi^{(1)})[\zeta]=\Phi\begin{pmatrix}-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot u\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta p+\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\nabla(\zeta\cdot u)+\mu 4\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\nabla u\\
\kappa 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\chi e_{3}-\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta(u\cdot e_{3})\\
2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot H\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta^{\perp}\cdot\chi\end{pmatrix}.

If
j=2
, we set

(4.26)

(p^{(2)},u^{(2)},\chi^{(2)})[\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}]\\
=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}}\Phi\begin{pmatrix}-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta_{\sigma 1}\cdot u^{(1)}[\zeta_{\sigma 2}]\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta_{\sigma 1}p^{(1)}[\zeta_{\sigma 2}]+\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\nabla(\zeta_{\sigma 1}\cdot u^{(1)}[\zeta_{\sigma 2}])+\mu 4\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta_{\sigma 1}\cdot\nabla u^{(1)}[\zeta_{\sigma 2}]-\mu 4\pi^{2}(\zeta_{\sigma 1}\cdot\zeta_{\sigma 2})u\\
\kappa 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta_{\sigma 1}\cdot\chi^{(1)}[\zeta_{\sigma 2}]e_{3}-\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta_{\sigma 1}(u^{(1)}[\zeta_{\sigma 2}]\cdot e_{3})\\
0\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta^{\perp}_{\sigma 1}\cdot{\chi^{(1)}}[\zeta_{\sigma 2}]\end{pmatrix}

and if
j\geqslant 3
, we take

(4.27)

(p^{(j)},u^{(j)},\chi^{(j)})[\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j}]\\
=\frac{1}{(j-1)!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{j}}\Phi\begin{pmatrix}-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta_{\sigma 1}\cdot u^{(j-1)}\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta_{\sigma 1}p^{(j-1)}+\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\nabla(\zeta_{\sigma 1}\cdot u^{(j-1)})+\mu 4\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta_{\sigma 1}\cdot\nabla u^{(j-1)}\\
\kappa 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta_{\sigma 1}\cdot\chi^{(j-1)}e_{3}-\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta_{\sigma 1}(u^{(j-1)}\cdot e_{3})\\
0\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta^{\perp}_{\sigma 1}\cdot\chi^{(j-1)}\end{pmatrix}[\zeta_{\sigma 2},\dots,\zeta_{\sigma j}]\\
+\frac{1}{(j-2)!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{j}}\Phi\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-\mu 4\pi^{2}(\zeta_{\sigma 1}\cdot\zeta_{\sigma 2})u^{(j-2)}\\
0\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}[\zeta_{\sigma 3},\dots,\zeta_{\sigma j}].

Our next result studies the previous construction more carefully.

Proposition 4.10

(Properties of the derivative construction)
.

The following hold.

(1)

For every
j\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
the map

(4.28)

\{{(f,k,H)\in\mathbb{Y}_{s}\;:\;0\not\in\supp\mathscr{F}(f,k,H)}\}\ni(f,k,H)\mapsto|D|^{j}(p^{(j)},u^{(j)},\chi^{(j)})\in\mathcal{L}^{j}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{X}_{s})

is continuous and tangentially translation commuting, and hence extends uniquely to a bounded linear map defined on all of
\mathbb{Y}_{s}
.

(2)

For every
j\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
there exists a unique multilinear mapping into the space of symbols,
(\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j})\mapsto\mathbf{m}^{(j)}[\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j}]
, such that for all
(f,k,H)\in\mathbb{Y}_{s}
satisfying
0\not\in\supp\mathscr{F}(f,k,H)
we have the identity

(4.29)

\mathbf{m}^{(j)}[\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j}](D)(f,k)=(p^{(j)},u^{(j)},\chi^{(j)})[\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j}].

(3)

For all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and all
(\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j})\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{j}
we have that the map
\xi\mapsto|\xi|^{j}\mathbf{m}^{(j)}[\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j}](\xi)
belongs to the space
\mathfrak{A}(s)
from Definition
4.2
; moreover, we have the estimate

(4.30)

\llbracket|\cdot|^{j}\mathbf{m}^{(j)}[\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j}]\rrbracket_{s}\lesssim j!\cdot C^{j}\prod_{i=1}^{j}|\zeta_{i}|,

with an implicit constant independent of
j
.

Proof.

We begin with the first item in the case
j=1
. The key point is that the operator norm of
\Phi:\widetilde{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}\to\mathbb{X}_{s}
depends on a few quantities belonging to
\dot{H}^{-1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C})
. The appearance of the operator
|D|
is thus crucial, as it permits the bounds

(4.31)

\Big[{2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot|D|H+2\pi\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{b}\zeta\cdot(|D|u)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y}\Big]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}+[{2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta^{\perp}\cdot|D|\chi}]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}\lesssim|\zeta|\lVert\chi,u,H\rVert_{L^{2}\times L^{2}\times L^{2}}.

With this observation in hand, we appeal directly to Definition
4.9
and the mapping properties of
\Phi
established in Theorem
2.6
and Definition
4.1
, to verify that

(4.32)

\lVert|D|(p^{(1)},u^{(1)},\chi^{(1)})[\zeta]\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\lesssim(\lVert p,u,\chi\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}+\lVert f,k,H\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}})|\zeta|\lesssim\lVert f,k,H\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}}|\zeta|.

In a similar manner to the above, we may derive the estimate

(4.33)

\sup_{|\zeta_{1}|,|\zeta_{2}|\leqslant 1}\lVert|D|^{2}(p^{(2)},u^{(2)},\chi^{(2)})[\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}]\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\lesssim\sup_{|\zeta|\leqslant 1}\lVert|D|(p^{(1)},u^{(1)},\chi^{(1)})[\zeta]\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}+\lVert p,u,\chi\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\lesssim\lVert f,k\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}}

and, for
j\geqslant 3
,

(4.34)

\sup_{|\zeta_{1}|,\dots,|\zeta_{j}|\leqslant 1}\lVert|D|^{j}(p^{(j)},u^{(j)},\chi^{(j)})[\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j}]\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\lesssim\\
j\sum_{\sigma=1}^{2}((j-2)\sigma+3-j)\sup_{|\zeta_{1}|,\dots,|\zeta_{j-\sigma}|\leqslant 1}\lVert|D|^{j-\sigma}(p^{(j-\sigma)},u^{(j-\sigma)},\chi^{(j-\sigma)})[\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j-\sigma}]\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}.

Upon iterating these estimates, we deduce the boundedness assertion of the first item.

As a consequence of the first item and Proposition
4.4
, we find that for each
j\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
(\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j})\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{j}
, the map
(f,k,H)\mapsto|D|^{j}(p^{(j)},u^{(j)},\chi^{(j)})[\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j}]
is given by a symbol
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}^{(j)}[\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j}]\in\mathfrak{A}(s)
. By uniqueness, we must have that
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}^{(j)}
is a
j
-multilinear and symmetric function of the
\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j}
. We then set
\mathbf{m}^{(j)}=|\cdot|^{-j}\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}^{(j)}
, which implies that (
4.29
) holds, and hence the second item is satisfied.

The third item is follows since
|\cdot|^{j}\mathbf{m}^{(j)}=\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}^{(j)}
, with the symbol on the right a
j
-multilinear function of the
\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{j}
into
\mathfrak{A}(s)
.
∎

We pause to note that estimate (
4.30
) would tell us that
\mathbf{m}

obeys estimates of Mikhlin-Hörmander type if we knew that
\mathbf{m}
were smooth away from the origin with the
j^{\mathrm{th}}
-derivative equal to
\mathbf{m}^{(j)}
. It is thus our goal now to prove that these are, in fact the derivatives of the symbol
\mathbf{m}
. We first require a definition and a technical lemma about remainders. Note that the following is well-defined thanks to Proposition
4.10
.

Definition 4.11

(Remainders)
.

Given
(f,k,H)\in\mathbb{Y}_{s}
satisfying
0\not\in\supp\mathscr{F}(f,k,H)
and
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we define the following elements of
\mathbb{X}_{s}
:

(4.35)

\mathcal{R}_{0}(f,k,H)[\zeta]=(\mathbf{m}(D+\zeta)-\mathbf{m}(D))(f,k),

and for
j\in\mathbb{N}^{+}

(4.36)

\mathcal{R}_{j}(f,k,H)[\zeta]=\mathcal{R}_{0}(f,k,H)[\zeta]-\sum_{i=1}^{j}\frac{1}{i!}\mathbf{m}^{(i)}[\zeta^{\otimes i}](D)(f,k,H),

where in the above we write
\zeta^{\otimes i}\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{i}
to refer to the
i
-tuple of vectors with each entry equal to
\zeta
.

Now we derive estimates for these remainder terms.

Lemma 4.12

(Remainder estimates)
.

There exists a constant
C\geqslant 0
such that for all
(f,k,H)\in\mathbb{Y}_{s}
satisfying
0\not\in\supp\mathscr{F}(f,k,H)
and
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying
|\zeta|<\min\{{\mathrm{dist}(0,\supp\mathscr{F}(f,k,H)),1}\}
, we have the following estimates for
j\in\mathbb{N}
:

(4.37)

\lVert|D|^{j+1}\mathcal{R}_{j}(f,k,H)[\zeta]\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\leqslant C^{j+1}|\zeta|^{j+1}\lVert f,k,H\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}}.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we will employ the following convenient notation: we set
(p_{\zeta},u_{\zeta},\chi_{\zeta})=\mathbf{m}(D+\zeta)(f,k,H)
, and for
j\in\mathbb{N}
we set

(4.38)

\mathcal{R}_{j}(f,k,H)[\zeta]=(\mathcal{R}_{j}p[\zeta],\mathcal{R}_{j}u[\zeta],\mathcal{R}_{j}\chi[\zeta]).

We first establish (
4.37
) when
j=0
. From identity (
4.22
), we find that

(4.39)

\mathcal{R}_{0}(f,k,H)[\zeta]=\Phi\begin{pmatrix}-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot u_{\zeta}\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta p_{\zeta}+\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\nabla(\zeta\cdot u_{\zeta})+\mu 4\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\nabla u_{\zeta}-\mu 4\pi^{2}|\zeta|^{2}u_{\zeta}\\
\kappa 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\chi_{\zeta}e_{3}-\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta(u_{\zeta}\cdot e_{3})\\
2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot H\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta^{\perp}\cdot\chi_{\zeta}\end{pmatrix}.

We then apply
|D|
to the above and take the norm in
\mathbb{X}_{s}
. Thanks to the continuity properties of
\Phi:\widetilde{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}\to\mathbb{X}_{s}
(see the proof of Proposition
4.10
) and Lemma
4.8
, we acquire the bound

(4.40)

\lVert|D|\mathcal{R}_{0}(f,k,H)[\zeta]\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\lesssim|\zeta|(\lVert p_{\zeta},u_{\zeta},\eta_{\zeta}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}+\lVert f,k,H\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}})\lesssim|\zeta|\lVert f,k,H\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}},

which completes the proof in the
j=0
case.

Next up is
j=1
. We subtract
\mathbf{m}^{(1)}[\zeta](D)(f,k,H)
from both sides of (
4.39
) and recall (
4.25
). This yields the equation

(4.41)

\mathcal{R}_{1}(f,k,H)[\zeta]=\Phi\begin{pmatrix}-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\mathcal{R}_{0}u[\zeta]\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\mathcal{R}_{0}p[\zeta]+\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\nabla(\zeta\cdot\mathcal{R}_{0}u[\zeta])+\mu 4\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\nabla\mathcal{R}_{0}u[\zeta]-\mu 4\pi^{2}|\zeta|^{2}u_{\zeta}\\
\kappa 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\mathcal{R}_{0}\chi[\zeta]e_{3}-\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta(\mathcal{R}_{0}u[\zeta]\cdot e_{3})\\
0\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta^{\perp}\cdot\mathcal{R}_{0}\chi[\zeta]\end{pmatrix}.

We now apply
|D|^{2}
to (
4.41
) and then take the norm in
\mathbb{X}_{s}
. The mapping properties of
\Phi
, together with Lemma
4.8
and estimate (
4.40
), then show that

(4.42)

\lVert|D|^{2}\mathcal{R}_{1}(f,k,H)[\zeta]\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\lesssim|\zeta|\lVert|D|\mathcal{R}_{0}(f,k,H)[\zeta]\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}+|\zeta|^{2}\lVert|D|^{2}u_{\zeta}\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim|\zeta|^{2}\lVert f,k\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}}.

This completes the proof in the case
j=1
.

Now we claim the following identity holds for all
j\geqslant 2
:

(4.43)

\mathcal{R}_{j}(f,k,H)[\zeta]=\Phi\begin{pmatrix}-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\mathcal{R}_{j-1}u[\zeta]\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\mathcal{R}_{j-1}p[\zeta]+\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\nabla(\zeta\cdot\mathcal{R}_{j-1}u[\zeta])+\mu 4\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\nabla\mathcal{R}_{j-1}u[\zeta]-\mu 4\pi^{2}|\zeta|^{2}\mathcal{R}_{j-2}u[\zeta]\\
\kappa 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\mathcal{R}_{j-1}\chi[\zeta]e_{3}-\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta(\mathcal{R}_{j-1}u[\zeta]\cdot e_{3})\\
0\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta^{\perp}\cdot\mathcal{R}_{j-1}\chi[\zeta]\end{pmatrix}.

We prove this via induction. For the base case
j=2
, we look to Definition
4.9
to see that

(4.44)

\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{m}^{(2)}[\zeta^{\otimes 2}](D)(f,k,H)=\begin{pmatrix}-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot u^{(1)}[\zeta]\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta p^{(1)}[\zeta]+\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\nabla(\zeta\cdot u^{(1)}[\zeta])+\mu 4\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\nabla u^{(1)}[\zeta]-\mu 4\pi^{2}|\zeta|^{2}u\\
\kappa 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\chi^{(1)}[\zeta]e_{3}-\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta(u^{(1)}[\zeta]\cdot e_{3})\\
0\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta^{\perp}\cdot\chi^{(1)}[\zeta]\end{pmatrix}.

We subtract the above from (
4.41
) to see that (
4.43
) is true for
j=2
. Proceeding inductively, suppose that this identity holds for some
\mathbb{N}\ni j\geqslant 2
. We again look to Definition
4.9
to acquire the identity

(4.45)

\frac{1}{(j+1)!}\mathbf{m}^{(j+1)}[\zeta^{\otimes(j+1)}](D)(f,k,H)\\
=\frac{1}{j!}\Phi\begin{pmatrix}-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot u^{(j)}[\zeta^{\otimes j}]\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta p^{(j)}[\zeta^{\otimes j}]+\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\nabla(\zeta\cdot u^{(j)}[\zeta^{\otimes(j)}])+\mu 4\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\nabla u^{(j)}[\zeta^{\otimes(j)}]\\
\kappa 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta\cdot\chi^{(j)}[\zeta^{\otimes(j)}]e_{3}-\mu 2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta(u^{(j)}[\zeta^{\otimes j}]\cdot e_{3})\\
0\\
-2\pi\mathrm{i}\zeta^{\perp}\cdot\chi^{(j)}[\zeta^{\otimes j}]e_{3}\end{pmatrix}\\
+\frac{1}{(j-1)!}\Phi\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-\mu 4\pi^{2}|\zeta|^{2}u^{(j-1)}[\zeta^{\otimes(j-1)}]\\
0\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

We then simply subtract (
4.45
) from the induction hypothesis (
4.43
) to prove the stated identity in the
j+1
case. Thus (
4.43
) holds for all
\mathbb{N}\ni j\geqslant 2
, and the claim is proved.

With the claim in hand, we apply
|D|^{j+1}
in the case
\mathbb{N}\ni j\geqslant 2
to identity (
4.43
), take the norm in
\mathbb{X}_{s}
, and utilize the mapping properties of
\Phi
to deduce the inductive estimate

(4.46)

\lVert|D|^{j+1}\mathcal{R}_{j}(f,k,H)[\zeta]\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\lesssim\sum_{\sigma=1}^{2}|\zeta|^{\sigma}\lVert|D|^{j-\sigma+1}\mathcal{R}_{j-\sigma}(f,k,H)[\zeta]\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}.

Iteratively applying (
4.46
) and employing (
4.42
) and (
4.40
), we then readily conclude that (
4.37
) holds.
∎

At last, we prove analyticity of
\mathbf{m}
away from the origin.

Theorem 4.13

(Analyticity of the symbol)
.

The symbol
\mathbf{m}\in\mathfrak{A}(s)
from Definition
4.6
has a representative that is analytic as a mapping

(4.47)

\mathbf{m}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{{0}\}\to\mathcal{L}(H^{s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}^{3})\times\mathbb{C}^{3}\times\mathbb{C}^{2};H^{1+s}((0,b);\mathbb{C})\times H^{2+s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}^{3})\times\mathbb{C}^{2}).

Moreover, the derivatives of the above symbol obey the following Mikhlin-Hörmander type bounds: for every
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
there exists
C_{\alpha}<\infty
such that

(4.48)

\llbracket|\cdot|^{|\alpha|}\partial^{\alpha}\mathbf{m}\rrbracket_{s}\leqslant C_{\alpha}.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that
\mathbf{m}
is analytic as claimed and that
\nabla^{j}\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{m}^{(j)}
for
j\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
. Indeed, once this is established, the bound (
4.48
) is a consequence of the third item of Proposition
4.10
and Definition
4.6
. We divide the proof into several steps.

Step 1 - Setup and basic estimates:

For the sake of brevity, define the Hilbert spaces
V_{0}=H^{s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}^{3})\times\mathbb{C}^{3}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
V_{1}=H^{1+s}((0,b);\mathbb{C})\times H^{2+s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}^{3})\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
. We will write
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1})
throughout the proof.

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1/4)
and define the annuli
A_{2}=B(0,2\varepsilon^{-1})\setminus\overline{B(0,\varepsilon/2)}
,
A_{1}=B(0,\varepsilon^{-1})\setminus\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\subset A_{2}
, and
A_{0}=B(0,\varepsilon^{-1}/2)\setminus\overline{B(0,2\varepsilon)}\subset A_{1}
. Thanks to Definition
4.11
, for
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
|\zeta|<\varepsilon/2
the linear map

(4.49)

\mathbb{Y}_{s}\ni(f,k,H)\overset{T_{j}^{\varepsilon}[\zeta]}{\mapsto}\mathcal{R}_{j}(f,k,H)[\zeta]\mathds{1}_{A_{2}}(D)(f,k)\in\mathbb{X}_{s}

is bounded, translation commuting, and satisfies
T_{j}^{\varepsilon}[\zeta]=\mathbf{m}_{j}^{\varepsilon}[\zeta](D)
for the symbol

(4.50)

\mathbf{m}_{j}^{\varepsilon}[\zeta](\xi)=\Big(\mathbf{m}(\xi+\zeta)-\sum_{i=0}^{j}\frac{1}{i!}\mathbf{m}^{(i)}[\zeta^{\otimes i}](\xi)\Big)\mathds{1}_{A_{2}}(\xi),

with the understanding that
\frac{1}{0!}\mathbf{m}^{(0)}[\zeta^{\otimes 0}](\xi)=\mathbf{m}(\xi)
. Lemma
4.12
provides the estimate

(4.51)

\lVert|D|^{j+1}T^{\varepsilon}_{j}[\zeta]\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{Y}_{s};\mathbb{X}_{s})}\leqslant C^{j+1}|\zeta|^{j+1},

and so Proposition
4.4
then yields multiplier bound

(4.52)

\llbracket|\cdot|^{j+1}\mathbf{m}^{\varepsilon}_{j}[\zeta]\rrbracket_{s}\leqslant C^{j+1}|\zeta|^{j+1}\text{ for all }\left|\zeta\right|<\varepsilon/2.

Now, since the multiplier
\mathbf{m}^{\varepsilon}_{j}
is supported in the set
A_{2}
, we are free to apply Lemma
4.3
and find that there exists a constant
c_{\varepsilon}
, depending only
\varepsilon
and
s
, such that

(4.53)

\esssup_{\xi\in A_{2}}|\xi|^{j+1}\Big\lVert\mathbf{m}(\xi+\zeta)-\sum_{i=0}^{j}\frac{1}{i!}\mathbf{m}^{(i)}[\zeta^{\otimes i}](\xi)\Big\rVert_{\mathcal{L}}\leqslant c_{\varepsilon}\llbracket|\cdot|^{j+1}\mathbf{m}_{j}^{\varepsilon}[\zeta]\rrbracket_{s}\leqslant c_{\varepsilon}C^{j+1}|\zeta|^{j+1},

where we recall that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(V_{0};V_{1})
, and we deduce from this that if
\left|\zeta\right|<\varepsilon/2
, then

(4.54)

\esssup_{\xi\in A_{2}}\Big\lVert\mathbf{m}(\xi+\zeta)-\sum_{i=0}^{j}\frac{1}{i!}\mathbf{m}^{(i)}[\zeta^{\otimes i}](\xi)\Big\rVert_{\mathcal{L}}\leqslant c_{\varepsilon}\Big(\frac{2C|\zeta|}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{j+1}.

A similar application of Definition
4.6
and Lemma
4.3
shows that

(4.55)

\esssup_{\xi\in A_{2}}\left\lVert\mathbf{m}(\xi)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}}\leqslant c_{\varepsilon}.

Step 2 - Lipschitz continuity in
A_{1}
:

We now aim to show that
\mathbf{m}:A_{1}\to\mathcal{L}
has a Lipschitz continuous representative. To this end, let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be such that
\varphi\geqslant 0
,
\int\varphi=1
, and
\supp(\varphi)\subseteq B(0,1)
. For
0<\delta<\varepsilon/4
and
\xi\in A_{1}
we define
\mathbf{n}_{\varepsilon}(\xi):V_{0}\to V_{1}
via

(4.56)

\mathbf{n}_{\delta}(\xi)v=\int_{B(0,\delta)}\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\varphi(\omega/\delta)\mathbf{m}(\xi-\omega)v\;\mathrm{d}\omega,

which is well-defined since
B(0,\delta)\ni\omega\mapsto\mathbf{m}(\xi-\omega)v\in V_{2}
is measurable and essentially bounded for each
v\in V_{0}
and
\xi\in A_{1}
. From this it’s easy to see that
\mathbf{n}_{\delta}(\xi)\in\mathcal{L}
for each
\xi\in A_{1}
and that
the induced map
\mathbf{n}_{\delta}:A_{1}\to\mathcal{L}
is continuous. In fact, the mollified sequence is uniformly Lipschitz in
\delta
. Indeed, from (
4.54
) with
j=0

we know that there exists a null set
N\subset A_{2}
such that if
\xi\in A_{1}
and
\omega\in B(0,\delta)
are such that
\xi-\omega\in A_{2}\backslash N
, then

(4.57)

\sup_{\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{V_{0}}\leqslant 1}\left\lVert\mathbf{m}(\xi-\omega+\eta)v-\mathbf{m}(\xi-\omega)v\right\rVert_{V_{1}}\leqslant\sup_{\theta\in A_{2}\backslash N}\left\lVert\mathbf{m}(\theta+\eta)-\mathbf{m}(\theta)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}}\leqslant\frac{2c_{\varepsilon}C}{\varepsilon}|\eta|.

Consequently, for any given
\xi\in A_{1}
and
\left|\eta\right|<\varepsilon/2
we have that

(4.58)

\sup_{\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{V_{0}}\leqslant 1}\left\lVert\mathbf{m}(\xi-\omega+\eta)v-\mathbf{m}(\xi-\omega)v\right\rVert_{V_{1}}\leqslant\frac{2c_{\varepsilon}C}{\varepsilon}|\eta|\text{ for a.e. }\omega\in B(0,\delta),

and hence

(4.59)

\lVert\mathbf{n}_{\delta}(\xi+\eta)-\mathbf{n}_{\delta}(\xi)\rVert_{\mathcal{L}}\leqslant\frac{2c_{\varepsilon}C}{\varepsilon}|\eta|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\varphi(\omega/\delta)\;\mathrm{d}\omega=\frac{2c_{\varepsilon}C}{\varepsilon}|\eta|.

Since
\xi\in A_{1}
was arbitrary, we deduce that

(4.60)

\sup_{\xi\in A_{1}}\lVert\mathbf{n}_{\delta}(\xi+\eta)-\mathbf{n}_{\delta}(\xi)\rVert_{\mathcal{L}}\leqslant\frac{2c_{\varepsilon}C}{\varepsilon}|\eta|

for all
\left|\eta\right|<\varepsilon/2
. By similar considerations, we may estimate

(4.61)

\lVert\mathbf{n}_{\delta}-\mathbf{m}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\ast}(A_{1};\mathcal{L})}\leqslant\frac{2c_{\varepsilon}C}{\varepsilon}\int_{B(0,\delta)}\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\varphi(\omega/\delta)|\omega|\;\mathrm{d}\omega\leqslant\frac{2c_{\varepsilon}C}{\varepsilon}\delta.

Sending
\delta\to 0
and appealing to (
4.60
) and (
4.55
), we find that the restriction of
\mathbf{m}
to
A_{1}
is almost everywhere equal to a Lipschitz continuous function from
A_{1}
to
\mathcal{L}
. From now on, we shall use the continuous representative of
\mathbf{m}
in
A_{1}
.

Step 3 - Local convergence of the power series in
A_{2}
:

We now claim that there exists a constant
C_{1}\geqslant 2
and a set
\mathfrak{E}\subseteq A_{2}
with
|A_{2}\setminus\mathfrak{E}|=0
such that for any
\xi\in\mathfrak{E}
the power series

(4.62)

\mathbb{R}^{2}\supset B(\xi,\varepsilon/C_{1})\ni\zeta\mapsto\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{m}^{(j)}[\zeta^{\otimes j}](\xi)\in\mathcal{L}

converges uniformly absolutely and thus defines an analytic
\mathcal{L}
-valued function in
B(\xi,\varepsilon/N)
.

To prove the claim, we first appeal to the bounds (
4.30
) from Proposition
4.10
and Lemma
4.3
to find a constant
R>0
such that for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
we have the bound

(4.63)

\frac{1}{j!}\esssup_{\xi\in A_{2}}|\xi|^{j}\lVert\mathbf{m}^{(j)}[\zeta^{\otimes j}](\xi)\rVert_{\mathcal{L}}\leqslant c_{\varepsilon}R^{j}\char 12\relax{\zeta}^{j}\text{ for every }|\zeta|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

This estimate can be improved by virtue of multilinearity. To see how, set
C_{1}=2(1+2R)
and let
\{{\zeta_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset B(0,\varepsilon/C_{1})
be dense. Then by (
4.63
), the fact that a countable union of null sets is again null, the pointwise continuity of the multilinear maps involved, and the fact that
\mathrm{dist}(0,A_{2})=\varepsilon/2
, we have that

(4.64)

\esssup_{\xi\in A_{2}}\sup_{|\zeta|<\varepsilon/C_{1}}\lVert\mathbf{m}^{(j)}[\zeta^{\otimes j}](\xi)\rVert_{\mathcal{L}}\leqslant\esssup_{\xi\in A_{2}}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{2^{j}|\xi|^{j}}{\varepsilon^{j}}\lVert\mathbf{m}^{(j)}[\zeta_{n}^{\otimes j}](\xi)\rVert_{\mathcal{L}}\leqslant j!\cdot c_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{2R}{C_{1}}\right)^{j}\leqslant j!\cdot c_{\varepsilon}2^{-j}.

Summing over
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we may thus bound

(4.65)

\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}\esssup_{\xi\in A_{2}}\sup_{|\zeta|<\varepsilon/C_{1}}\lVert\mathbf{m}^{(j)}[\zeta^{\otimes j}](\xi)\rVert_{\mathcal{L}}\leqslant 2c_{\varepsilon},

and the claim now follows directly from this.

Step 4 - Analyticity in
A_{0}
:

Finally, we aim to prove that the multiplier
\mathbf{m}
in (
4.47
) is analytic in the annulus
A_{0}
. The strategy is to combine estimates (
4.65
) and (
4.54
). Let
C_{2}=\max\{C_{1},2(1+2C)\}
, where
C_{1}
is from Step 3 and
C>0
is the constant in (
4.54
). By letting
\{{\zeta_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset B(0,\varepsilon/C_{2})
be dense, we may use (
4.54
) to produce a set
\mathfrak{F}\subseteq A_{2}
with the property that
|A_{2}\setminus\mathfrak{F}|=0
and if
\xi\in\mathfrak{F}
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
then

(4.66)

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Big\lVert\mathbf{m}(\xi+\zeta_{n})-\sum_{i=0}^{j}\mathbf{m}^{(i)}[\zeta_{n}^{\otimes i}](\xi)\Big\rVert_{\mathcal{L}}\leqslant c_{\varepsilon}2^{-(j+1)}.

In particular, if
\xi\in A_{0}\cap\mathfrak{E}
, then the continuity assertion of Step 2 allows us to bound

(4.67)

\sup_{|\zeta|<\varepsilon/C_{2}}\Big\lVert\mathbf{m}(\xi+\zeta)-\sum_{i=0}^{j}\mathbf{m}^{(i)}[\zeta^{\otimes i}](\xi)\Big\rVert_{\mathcal{L}}\leqslant c_{\varepsilon}2^{-(j+1)}.

Let
\mathfrak{F}
be the set from Step 3 and note that
A_{0}\cap\mathfrak{E}\cap\mathfrak{F}
is a set of full measure in
A_{0}
. We can then send
j\to\infty
in (
4.67
) to see that

(4.68)

\mathbf{m}(\xi+\zeta)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{m}^{(i)}[\zeta^{\otimes i}](\xi)\text{ for }\xi\in A_{0}\cap\mathfrak{E}\cap\mathfrak{F}\text{ and }\zeta\in B(0,\varepsilon/C_{2}).

In light of the continuity of
\mathbf{m}
in
A_{1}
, we learn from this that the power series produced in Step 3 agree on the intersection of their balls of convergence. Consequently, we may produce a single
\mathcal{L}-
valued analytic function on
A_{0}
that is equal to
\mathbf{m}
a.e., and since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary we conclude that
\mathbf{m}
has an analytic representative in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
. Finally, we learn from (
4.68
) that
\nabla^{j}\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{m}^{(j)}
almost everywhere.

∎

5.
On some Sobolev-type spaces

In Section
4
we constructed an operator-valued symbol
\mathbf{m}
such that the corresponding Fourier multiplication operator
\mathbf{m}(D)
is a particular solution map for the PDE (
2.2
). We know from Theorem
4.13
that
\mathbf{m}
obeys certain inequalities of Mikhlin-Hörmander type, and so we expect to be able to employ Theorem
3.14
to obtain the boundedness of
\mathbf{m}(D)
on certain vector-valued Sobolev spaces. The first goal of this section is to define and study these mixed-type spaces for use in this manner. This is done in Section
5.1
. In Section
5.2
we record a number of nonlinear tools that we will use in working with the mixed-type spaces.

The second purpose of this section, which is the content of Section
5.3
, is to study what we call subcritical gradient spaces. These are spaces of functions whose distributional derivatives belong to
H^{s-1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
1<p<d
and
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, and they arise naturally in our analysis of the free surface function in (
1.13
). Their properties will play a crucial role in our subsequent PDE analysis.

Throughout this section, we have phrased the results in a more general manner than what is precisely needed in Sections
6
and
7
, as we believe that the analysis here may be of independent interest.

5.1.
Mixed-type Sobolev spaces

Throughout this subsection we consider a generic open interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
and define the set
U=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times I
for
d\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
.

Definition 5.1

(Mixed-type Sobolev spaces)
.

Let
1<p<\infty
and
V
be a finite dimensional normed space over
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
. Let
U=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times I
for
d\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
.

(1)

We define the mixed type Lebesgue space

(5.1)

L_{p,2}(U;V)=L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};L^{2}(I;V)),\hskip 10.00002pt\lVert f\rVert_{L_{p,2}}=\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(\int_{I}\lVert f(x,y)\rVert_{V}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{p/2}\;\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p},

which is a Banach space when endowed with the obvious norm. Moreover, the Fubini-Tonelli theorem shows that
L_{p,2}(U;V)\hookrightarrow L^{\min\{2,p\}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;V)
. We model Sobolev spaces on these mixed Lebesgue-spaces in the natural way.

(2)

For
s\in\mathbb{N}
we define

(5.2)

H^{s}_{p,2}(U;V)=\{{f\in L_{p,2}(U;V)\;:\;\partial^{\alpha}f\in L_{p,2}(U;V)\text{ for all }\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{3}\text{ with }|\alpha|\leqslant s}\},

and endow this space with the norm

(5.3)

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{s}_{p,2}}=\Big(\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leqslant s}\lVert\partial^{\alpha}f\rVert_{L_{p,2}}^{p}\Big)^{1/p}.

Minor variants of the usual Sobolev-theoretic arguments apply to show that these spaces are Banach and that the restrictions to
U
of elements of
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1};V)
form a dense subspace. When we write
H^{s}_{p,2}(U)
the understanding is that
V=\mathbb{R}
.

Our first lemma about these spaces provides a useful equivalent norm obtained via factorization.

Lemma 5.2

(Equivalent norm on the mixed type Sobolev spaces)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
1<p<\infty
, and
V
be a finite dimensional normed space over
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
. Then we have that

(5.4)

H^{s}_{p,2}(U;V)=L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};H^{s}(I;V))\cap H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};L^{2}(I;V))

with norm equivalence

(5.5)

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{s}_{p,2}}\asymp\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{p}H^{s}}+\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{s,p}L^{2}}.

Proof.

Since we can always complexify a real normed space
V
, it suffices to prove the result when
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
. Assume this. The continuous embedding of the space of the left of (
5.4
) into the right is obvious; the reverse inclusion requires work. Suppose that
f
belongs to the space on the right and
k\in\{{1,\dots,s-1}\}
. Let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a generator for a homogeneous Littlewood-Paley partition of unity. We then use Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation on the space
H^{k}(I;V)\hookrightarrow H^{s}(I;V)
, together with Young’s inequality for products, namely
a^{1-k/s}b^{k/s}\lesssim a+b
, the triangle inequality, and Theorem
3.10
to estimate

(5.6)

\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2(s-k))}\lVert\varphi(D/2^{j})f\rVert^{2}_{H^{k}}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}\\
\lesssim\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2(s-k)}\lVert\varphi(D/2^{j})f\rVert^{2(1-k/s)}_{L^{2}}\lVert\varphi(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2s/k}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}\\
\lesssim\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2s}\lVert\varphi(D/2^{j})f\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}+\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\lVert\varphi(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s,p}L^{2}}+\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}H^{s}}.

In turn, we may use Theorem
3.11
to conclude that
f=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi(D/2^{j})f
and

(5.7)

\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s-k,p}H^{k}}\lesssim\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2(s-k))}\lVert\varphi(D/2^{j})f\rVert^{2}_{H^{k}}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s,p}L^{2}}+\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}H^{s}}.

Summing over
k\in\{1,\dotsc,s-1\}
then completes the proof of the reverse embedding in (
5.4
).
∎

Next we turn our attention to extension operators.

Proposition 5.3

(Extensions on mixed-type Sobolev spaces)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
1<p<\infty
, and
V
be a finite dimensional normed space over
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
. There exists a bounded linear extension operator

(5.8)

\mathfrak{E}_{U}:H^{s}_{p,2}(U;V)\to H^{s}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1};V)

such that
\mathfrak{R}_{U}\mathfrak{E}_{U}=\mathrm{id}
on
H^{s}_{p,2}(U)
, where
\mathfrak{R}_{U}
denotes the restriction operator.

Proof.

Since
V
is assumed to be finite dimensional over
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
, we may use a basis to reduce to the case
V=\mathbb{F}
. The notion of a Stein extension operator is given in Section 3.1 in Chapter VI of Stein
[
87
]
. For the domain
U=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times I
, one can select a Stein-extension operator that is tangentially translation commuting. In fact, we only need the Stein-extension operator
I\to\mathbb{R}
and view it as acting on functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times I
via carrying along the first
d
-variables as parameters. It is then immediate that

(5.9)

\mathfrak{E}_{U}:L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};H^{s}(I;\mathbb{F}))\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};H^{s}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{F}))

is a bounded linear map. From tangential translation invariance, we get

(5.10)
