\lVert\mathfrak{E}_{U}f\rVert_{H^{s,p}L^{2}}=\lVert\mathfrak{E}_{U}\langle D\rangle^{s}f\rVert_{L^{p}L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\lVert\langle D\rangle^{s}f\rVert_{L^{2}(I)}\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s,p}L^{2}}\text{ for }f\in H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};L^{2}(I;\mathbb{F})),

and hence
\mathfrak{E}_{U}:H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};L^{2}(I;\mathbb{F}))\to H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{F}))
is bounded. Thus, (
5.8
) follows from Lemma
5.2
.
∎

Next, we discuss traces. Note that in this result the regularity loss caused by taking a trace is
1/2
rather than
1/p
. This is due to the
L^{2}-
based regularity spaces used in the ‘normal’ direction.

Proposition 5.4

(Traces of mixed-type Sobolev spaces)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
1<p<\infty
, and
V
be a finite dimensional normed space over
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
. For
b\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
define
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,b)
and
\Sigma=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{b\}
. Then there exists a bounded and linear trace map

(5.11)

\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}:H^{s}_{p,2}(\Omega;V)\to H^{s-1/2,p}(\Sigma;V).

Proof.

Using a basis of
V
, we reduce to proving the result with
V=\mathbb{F}
. The key observation is the following interpolation inequality for functions
\phi\in H^{s}((0,b))
:

(5.12)

|\phi(b)|\lesssim\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{1-1/2s}\lVert\phi\rVert_{H^{s}}^{1/2s},

where the implicit constant depends on
b
and
s
. A proof may be found, for instance, in Lemmas 4.9 and 4.10 of Constantin and Foias
[
29
]
.

Let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
generate a homogeneous Littlewood-Paley partition of unity. Then, given
f\in H^{s}_{p,2}(\Omega;\mathbb{F})
we use the above interpolation inequality with Young’s inequality, namely
a^{1-1/2s}b^{1/2s}\lesssim a+b
, and Theorem
3.10
to bound

(5.13)

\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2s-1}|\varphi(D/2^{j})\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}f|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}\lesssim\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2s-1}\lVert\varphi(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2-1/s}\lVert\varphi(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{H^{s}}^{1/s}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}\\
\lesssim\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2s}\lVert\varphi(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}+\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\lVert\varphi(D/2^{j})f\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}H^{s}}+\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s,p}L^{2}}.

This, Lemma
5.2
, and Theorem
3.11
then provide the estimate

(5.14)

\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}f\rVert_{H^{s-1/2,p}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}H^{s}}+\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s,p}L^{2}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{s}_{p,2}},

so the trace operator is bounded as stated.
∎

Now we discuss lifting maps that complement the trace map.

Proposition 5.5

(Lifting in mixed-type Sobolev spaces)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, and
1<p<\infty
, and
V
be a finite dimensional normed space over
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
. For
b\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
let
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,b)
and
\Sigma=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{b\}
. There exists a bounded linear extension map
\mathrm{L}_{\Omega}:H^{s-1/2,p}(\Sigma;V)\to H^{s}_{2,p}(\Omega;V)
such that
\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}\mathrm{L}_{\Omega}=\mathrm{id}_{H^{s-1/2,p}(\Sigma)}
.

Proof.

We will prove the result when
V=\mathbb{C}
; the general case can be deduced from this. Given
f\in\mathscr{S}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C})
, we define
\mathrm{L}_{\Omega}f:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}
via

(5.15)

\mathscr{F}[\mathrm{L}_{\Omega}f](\xi,y)=\exp(\langle\xi\rangle(y-b))\mathscr{F}[f](\xi)\text{ for }\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ and }y\in(0,b).

Now define
m\in C^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{C})
via
m(\xi,y)=\exp(\langle\xi\rangle(y-b))
. Thanks to the Leibniz rule and Faà di Bruno’s formula, we have that

(5.16)

|\partial_{y}^{j}D^{k}_{\xi}m(\xi,y)|=|D^{k}_{\xi}(\langle\xi\rangle^{j}\exp(\langle\xi\rangle(y-b)))|\lesssim\sum_{i=0}^{k}\langle\xi\rangle^{j-k+i}|D^{i}_{\xi}(\exp(\langle\xi\rangle(y-b)))|\\
\lesssim\sum_{i=0}^{k}\langle\xi\rangle^{j-k+i}\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}\langle\xi\rangle^{-i+\ell}|y-b|^{\ell}\exp(\langle\xi\rangle(y-b))\lesssim\langle\xi\rangle^{j-k}\max_{1\leqslant\ell\leqslant k}|y-b|^{\ell}\langle\xi\rangle^{\ell}\exp(\langle\xi\rangle(y-b)).

We readily deduce from this that for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
there exists a constant
C_{\alpha}>0
such that

(5.17)

\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle\xi\rangle^{|\alpha|+1/2}\lVert\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}m(\xi,\cdot)\rVert_{L^{2}}+\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle\xi\rangle^{|\alpha|+1/2-s}\lVert\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}m(\xi,\cdot)\rVert_{H^{s}}\leqslant C_{\alpha}.

Let
\ell\in\{0,s\}
. Employing the canonical isometric identification
H^{\ell}((0,b);\mathbb{C})=\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C};H^{\ell}((0,b);\mathbb{C}))
, we define the vector-valued Fourier multiplier with symbol
\mu\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C};H^{\ell}((0,b);\mathbb{C})))
given by
\mu(\xi)=m(\xi,\cdot)
. In light of (
5.17
), we can then invoke Theorem
3.14
twice to deduce that

(5.18)

\mu(D):H^{s-1/2,p}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C})\to H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};L^{2}((0,b);\mathbb{C}))\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};H^{s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}))=H^{s}_{2,p}(\Omega;\mathbb{C})

is a bounded linear map (in the last equality we have employed Lemma
5.2
). To conclude, we simply note that for
f\in\mathscr{S}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C})
we have that
\mu(D)f(x)=\mathrm{L}_{\Omega}f(x,\cdot)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
∎

Finally, we record a mixed-type Sobolev spaces variant of Proposition C.1 in Stevenson and Tice
[
90
]
.

Proposition 5.6

(Divergence compatibility in mixed-type Sobolev spaces)
.

Let
1<p<\infty
,
b\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,b)
. Then there exists a constant
C
such that for all
u\in H^{1}_{p,2}(\Omega;\mathbb{F}^{d+1})
we have the estimate

(5.19)

\Big[{\int_{0}^{b}(\nabla\cdot u)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}u\cdot e_{d+1}+\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}u\cdot e_{d+1}}\Big]_{\dot{H}^{-1,p}}\leqslant C\lVert u\rVert_{L_{p,2}}.

Proof.

By density, it suffices to consider the case that
0\not\in\mathrm{supp}\mathscr{F}[u]
. Thanks to the fundamental theorem of calculus, we have

(5.20)

\int_{0}^{b}(\nabla\cdot u)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}u\cdot e_{d+1}+\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}u\cdot e_{d+1}=(\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{d},0)\cdot\int_{0}^{b}u(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y

Applying
|D|^{-1}
and using the definition of
\dot{H}^{-1,p}
from (
1.28
) and (
1.29
) along with the boundedness of Riesz transforms yields

(5.21)

\Big[{\int_{0}^{b}(\nabla\cdot u)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}u\cdot e_{d+1}+\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}u\cdot e_{d+1}}\Big]_{\dot{H}^{-1,p}}\lesssim\Big\lVert\int_{0}^{b}u(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}.

We conclude after noting that the embedding
L^{2}((0,b))\hookrightarrow L^{1}((0,b))
allows us to bound the right hand side of (
5.21
) by
\lVert u\rVert_{L_{p,2}}
.
∎

5.2.
Some nonlinear analysis in mixed-type Sobolev spaces

The goal of this subsection is to record a series of useful results related to the nonlinear use of mixed-type spaces. As in Section
5.1
, we will let
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be an open interval and set
U=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times I
.

Our first result gives a product estimates for the mixed-type Sobolev spaces.

Lemma 5.7

(Product estimates in mixed type Sobolev spaces)
.

Suppose
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
1<p<\infty
, and
V
is a finite dimensional normed space over
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
. Then we have the estimate

(5.22)

\lVert fg\rVert_{H^{s}_{p,2}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L_{p,2}}\lVert g\rVert_{H^{s}_{p,2}}+\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}_{p,2}}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L_{p,2}}

for all
f\in H^{s}_{p,2}(U;\mathbb{F})\cap L^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{F})
and
g\in H^{s}_{p,2}(U;V)\cap L^{\infty}(U;V)
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the result for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
, so we will assume this in the proof.
To check the product belongs to the correct space, we will use the norm from Lemma
5.2
. We first recall the well-known (see e.g. Theorem D.6 in
[
90
]
) high-low product estimate

(5.23)

\left\lVert FG\right\rVert_{H^{s}(I;V)}\lesssim\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(I;\mathbb{F})}\left\lVert G\right\rVert_{H^{s}(I;V)}+\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{H^{s}(I;\mathbb{F})}\left\lVert G\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(I;V)}

for all
F\in H^{s}(I;\mathbb{F})\cap L^{\infty}(I;\mathbb{F})
and
G\in H^{s}(I;V)\cap L^{\infty}(I;V)
. Applying this almost everywhere in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and integrating, we then derive the bound

(5.24)

\lVert fg\rVert_{L^{p}H^{s}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{p}H^{s}}+\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}H^{s}}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{\infty}}.

The bounds for tangential derivatives are more involved. To handle them we let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be generator for a homogeneous Littlewood-Paley partition of unity, we set
\Phi=\sum_{j\leqslant 0}\varphi(\cdot/2^{j})
, and we introduce the (tangential, homogeneous) paraproduct decomposition

(5.25)

fg=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi(D/2^{j})f\;\Phi(D/2^{j-3})g+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{k=j-2}^{j+2}\varphi(D/2^{j})f\;\varphi(D/2^{k})g+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\Phi(D/2^{k-3})f\;\varphi(D/2^{k})g\\
=\pi_{\mathrm{hl}}(f,g)+\pi_{\mathrm{hh}}(f,g)+\pi_{\mathrm{lh}}(f,g).

For the
\pi_{\mathrm{hl}}
term, we use the annular Littlewood-Paley estimates from Theorems
3.10
and
3.11
together with the bound

(5.26)

\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\Phi(D/2^{j})g\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{L^{\infty}},

which follows from Young’s convolution inequality, in order to estimate

(5.27)

\lVert\pi_{\mathrm{hl}}(f,g)\rVert_{H^{s,p}L^{2}}\lesssim\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle 2^{j}\rangle^{2s}\lVert\varphi(D/2^{j})f\;\Phi(D/2^{j-3})g\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s,p}L^{2}}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{\infty}}.

By an entirely symmetric argument, we have that
\lVert\pi_{\mathrm{lh}}(f,g)\rVert_{H^{s,p}L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert g\rVert_{H^{s,p}L^{2}}
. For the remaining high-high paraproduct, we split again:

(5.28)

\pi_{\mathrm{hh}}(f,g)=\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{k=j-2}^{j+2}+\sum_{j<0}\sum_{k=j-2}^{j+2}\Big)\varphi(D/2^{j})f\varphi(D/2^{k})g=\pi_{\mathrm{hhh}}(f,g)+\pi_{\mathrm{hhl}}(f,g).

We now use the ball Littlewood-Paley estimate of Theorem
3.12
to handle
\pi_{\mathrm{hhh}}
:

(5.29)

\lVert\pi_{\mathrm{hhh}}(f,g)\rVert_{H^{s,p}L^{2}}\lesssim\sum_{|c|\leqslant 2}\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}4^{sj}\lVert\varphi(D/2^{j})f\;\varphi(D/2^{j+c})g\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s,p}L^{2}}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{\infty}},

where in the final inequality we have pulled
g
out in
L^{\infty}(U;V)
and estimated
f
in
H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};L^{2}(I;\mathbb{F}))
as above. To handle
\pi_{\mathrm{hhl}}
we first note that it is band limited; indeed, it is supported in
B(0,16)
by construction. Hence the
L^{p}L^{2}
norm controls
H^{s,p}L^{2}
:

(5.30)

\lVert\pi_{\mathrm{hhl}}(f,g)\rVert_{H^{s,p}L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\pi_{\mathrm{hhl}}(f,g)\rVert_{L^{p}L^{2}}\leqslant\sum_{|c|\leqslant 2}\Big\lVert\sum_{j<0}\varphi(D/2^{j})f\varphi(D/2^{j+c})g\Big\rVert_{L^{p}L^{2}}.

For the final term above, we then use Cauchy-Schwartz, Hölder, and Theorem
3.10
:

(5.31)

\Big\lVert\sum_{j<0}\varphi(D/2^{j})f\varphi(D/2^{j+c})g\Big\rVert_{L^{p}L^{2}}\lesssim\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j<0}\lVert\varphi(D/2^{j})f\rVert^{2}_{H^{1}}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\sum_{j<0}\lVert\varphi(D/2^{j+c})g\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{p}}\\
\lesssim\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j<0}\lVert\varphi(D/2^{j})\Phi(D/2^{3})f\rVert_{H^{1}}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{2p}}\Big\lVert\Big(\sum_{j<0}\lVert\varphi(D/2^{j})\Phi(D/2^{3+c})g\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big\rVert_{L^{2p}}\\
\lesssim\lVert\Phi(D/2^{3})f\rVert_{L^{2p}H^{1}}\lVert\Phi(D/2^{3+c})g\rVert_{L^{2p}L^{2}}.

Now we invoke Young’s inequality and the fact that
\Phi(D/2^{3})f
and
\Phi(D/2^{3+c})g
are given via (tangential) convolution with a Schwartz function to bound

(5.32)

\lVert\Phi(D/2^{3})f\rVert_{L^{2p}H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}H^{1}}\text{ and }\lVert\Phi(D/2^{3+c})g\rVert_{L^{2p}L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{L^{p}L^{2}}.

Upon synthesizing these estimates, we arrive at

(5.33)

\lVert\pi_{\mathrm{hhl}}(f,g)\rVert_{H^{s,p}L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}H^{1}}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{p}L^{2}}.

Since
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 1
, we see that this completes the proof.
∎

As a consequence of the previous result and a supercritical embedding, we find sufficient conditions under which the mixed-type Sobolev spaces are an algebra.

Proposition 5.8

(Supercritical Sobolev embeddings in mixed-type Sobolev spaces)
.

The following hold.

(1)

H^{s}_{p,2}(U;\mathbb{F})\hookrightarrow C^{k}_{0}(U;\mathbb{F})
for
s>k+(d+1)/\min\{{2,p}\}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

(2)

For
s>(d+1)/\min\{{2,p}\}
, the mixed type Sobolev
H^{s}_{p,2}(U;\mathbb{F})
space is an algebra.

Proof.

For the first item we note that the restriction
H^{s}_{p,2}(U;\mathbb{F})\to W^{s,\min\{{2,p}\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times J;\mathbb{F})
is continuous, for every
J\subset I
of finite length and hence the claim follows from standard Sobolev embeddings. The second item follows from the first and Lemma
5.7
.
∎

We now rapidly record three useful consequences of the previous results.

Proposition 5.9

(Products, I)
.

Suppose that
1<p<\infty
and
\mathbb{N}\ni s>(d+1)/\min\{{2,p}\}
. Then the pointwise product map

(5.34)

H^{s}_{p,2}(U)\times H^{s}_{p,2}(U)\times W^{s,\infty}(U)\ni(f,g,h)\mapsto f\cdot(g+h)\in H^{s}_{p,2}(U)

is well-defined and smooth.

Proof.

That
H^{s}_{p,2}(U)\times W^{s,\infty}(U)\ni(f,h)\mapsto fh\in H^{s}_{p,2}(U)
is well-defined and smooth is clear from bilinearity and the Leibniz rule. On the other hand, that these properties as also true for the assignment
H^{s}_{p,2}(U)\times H^{s}_{p,2}(U)\ni(f,g)\mapsto fg\in H^{s}_{p,2}(U)
is a consequence of Lemma
5.7
and Proposition
5.8
.
∎

Proposition 5.10

(Products, II)
.

Suppose that
1<p<\infty
and
\mathbb{N}\ni s>(d+1)/\min\{{2,p}\}
. Then the Banach sum space

(5.35)

(H^{s}_{p,2}+W^{s,\infty})(U)=\{{f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)\;:\;f=f_{0}+f_{1},\;f_{0}\in H^{s}_{p,2}(U),\;f_{1}\in W^{s,\infty}(U)}\},

equipped with the norm

(5.36)

\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}_{2,p}+W^{s,\infty}}=\inf\{{\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{H^{s}_{2,p}}+\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{W^{s,\infty}}\;:\;f=f_{0}+f_{1}}\},

is a Banach algebra under pointwise multiplication.

Proof.

That this space is Banach is straightforward to see, so we only prove that it is an algebra. Let
f,g\in(H^{s}_{p,2}+W^{s,\infty})(U)
and decompose
f=f_{0}+f_{1}
,
g=g_{0}+g_{1}
with
f_{0},g_{0}\in H^{s}_{p,2}(U)
and
f_{1},g_{1}\in W^{s,\infty}(U)
. Then we use that
W^{s,\infty}(U)
is an algebra along with Proposition
5.9
to estimate

(5.37)

\lVert fg\rVert_{H^{s}_{p,2}+W^{s,\infty}}\leqslant\lVert f_{0}g_{0}+f_{1}g_{0}+f_{0}g_{1}\rVert_{H^{s}_{p,2}}+\lVert f_{1}g_{1}\rVert_{W^{s,\infty}}\lesssim(\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{H^{s}_{p,2}}+\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{W^{s,\infty}})(\lVert g_{0}\rVert_{H^{s}_{p,2}}+\lVert g_{1}\rVert_{W^{s,\infty}}).

The result follows by taking the infimum over all decompositions of
f
and
g
.
∎

Remark 5.11

(Products, III)
.

For
1<p<\infty
and
\mathbb{N}\ni s>(d+1)/\min\{{2,p}\}
, we have that the pointwise product map

(5.38)

H^{s}_{p,2}(U)\times(H^{s}_{p,2}+W^{s,\infty})(U)\ni(f,g)\mapsto fg\in H^{s}_{p,2}(U)

is smooth. This follows directly from Proposition
5.9
and definitions (
5.35
) and (
5.36
).

Our next result is meant to handle the reciprocal Jacobian of the flattening map.

Corollary 5.12

(Smoothness of pointwise inversion)
.

For
1<p<\infty
and
\mathbb{N}\ni s>(d+1)/\min\{{2,p}\}
, we have that there exists a constant
\rho\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, depending on
U
,
s
,
d
, and
p
, such that the map

(5.39)

(H^{s}_{p,2}+W^{s,\infty})(U)\supset B(0,\rho)\ni f\mapsto(1+f)^{-1}\in(H^{s}_{p,2}+W^{s,\infty})(U)

is well-defined and smooth.

Proof.

Proposition
5.10
established that
(H^{s}_{p,2}+W^{s,\infty})(U)
is a Banach algebra. Consequently, we can use the usual theory of power series on Banach algebras to pick
\rho
sufficiently small such that the power series

(5.40)

B(0,\rho)\ni f\mapsto\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}f^{j}

is uniformly absolutely convergent and defines an analytic map. It is then elementary to verify that this power series is pointwise equal to
f\mapsto(1+f)^{-1}
.
∎

Now we study the composition appearing in the interaction between the flattening map and the data. The following is a modification of the main argument presented in Inci, Kappeler, and Topalov
[
50
]
.

Proposition 5.13

(On composition)
.

Assume that
2\leqslant d\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
1<p<\infty
and
\mathbb{N}\ni s>1+d/\min\{{2,p}\}
. There exists a constant
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, depending only on
p
,
d
, and
s
such that the following hold.

(1)

If
f\in B_{(H^{s}_{p,2}+W^{s,\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(0,\lambda)
, then the map
\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}+fe_{d}
is a
C^{m}
diffeomorphism of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
onto itself for every
\mathbb{N}\ni m<s-d/\min\{{2,p}\}
.

(2)

If
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then the map
\Lambda
defined by

(5.41)

H^{s+k}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times B_{(H^{s}_{p,2}+W^{s,\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(0,\lambda)\ni(F,f)\mapsto\Lambda(F,f)=F(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}+fe_{d})\in H^{s}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is well-defined and
C^{k}
.

Proof.

Thanks to Corollary
5.12
, there exists a
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
for which the map

(5.42)

B_{(H^{s}_{p,2}+W^{s,\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(0,\lambda)\ni f\mapsto K_{f}=(1+\partial_{d}f)^{-1}\in(H^{s-1}_{p,2}+W^{s-1})(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is analytic, and
K_{f}>0
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for each
f
in the domain of the map. In turn, from the above and the fact that
s-1>d/\min\{{2,p}\}
, we find that the map
\mathfrak{F}_{f}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
given by
\mathfrak{F}_{f}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}+fe_{d}
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism; indeed the condition that
1+\partial_{d}f
is bounded and bounded away from zero guarantees that for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the map
\mathbb{R}\ni y\overset{\psi_{f,x}}{\mapsto}y+f(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}
is a diffeomorphism
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. Thus
\mathfrak{F}_{f}^{-1}(x,y)=(x,\psi_{f,x}^{-1}(y))
, for
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
.

That the maps
\mathfrak{F}_{f}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{f}^{-1}
are class
C^{m}
for every
\mathbb{N}\ni m<s-d/\min\{{2,p}\}
follows from Proposition
5.8
and the inverse function theorem. This completes the proof of the first item.

With
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
in hand, we now turn to the proof of the second item. First, we prove (
5.41
) in the case
k=0
via an induction argument. For
j\in\{{0,1,\dots,s}\}
let
\mathbb{P}_{j}
denote the proposition that

(5.43)

\Lambda:H^{j}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times B_{(H^{s}_{p,2}+W^{s,\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(0,\lambda)\to H^{j}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is well-defined and continuous.

For
\mathbb{P}_{0}
, we perform a change of variables
z=y+f(x,y)
on each fiber to estimate

(5.44)

\lVert\Lambda(F,f)\rVert_{H^{0}_{p,2}}=\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|F(x,y+f(x,y))|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{p/2}\;\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}\leqslant\lVert K_{f}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{1/2}\lVert F\rVert_{H^{0}_{p,2}}.

Thus well-definedness is established. For continuity, we estimate

(5.45)

\lVert\Lambda(F,f)-\Lambda(G,g)\rVert_{H^{0}_{p,2}}\leqslant\lVert\Lambda(F-G,f)\rVert_{H^{0}_{p,2}}+\lVert\Lambda(G,f)-\Lambda(G,g)\rVert_{H^{0}_{p,2}}.

The former term is made small when
(G,g)
are close to
(F,f)
via estimate (
5.44
), while the latter term is made small by approximating
G
via a smooth compactly supported function and using that
g\to f
in
(H^{s}_{p,2}+W^{s,\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{d})
implies that
\mathfrak{F}_{g}\to\mathfrak{F}_{f}
uniformly. This gives
\mathbb{P}_{0}
.

Now suppose that
j\in\{{0,\dots,s-1}\}
is such that
\mathbb{P}_{\ell}
is true for all
\ell\in\{{1,\dots,j}\}
. We compute

(5.46)

\partial_{q}(\Lambda(F,f))=\Lambda(\partial_{q}F,f)+\Lambda(\partial_{d}F,f)\partial_{q}f.

for
q\in\{{1,\dotsc,d}\}
. By combining Remark
5.11
and the induction hypothesis, we have that

(5.47)

H^{j+1}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times B_{(H^{s}_{p,2}+W^{s,\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(0,\lambda)\ni(F,f)\mapsto\partial_{q}(\Lambda(F,f))\in H^{j}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is a well-defined and continuous map. This paired with
\mathbb{P}_{0}
gives
\mathbb{P}_{j+1}
. Thus the induction is complete, and we have proved (
5.41
) in the case
k=0
.

We now turn to the proof of (
5.41
) for the remaining cases of
k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
. For this we shall use the converse to Taylor’s theorem: see, for instance, Theorem 2.4.15 and Supplement 2.4B in Abraham, Marsden, and Ratiu
[
11
]
. For
r\in\{{1,\dots,k}\}
, we define

(5.48)

\Lambda^{(r)}:H^{s+k}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times B_{(H^{s}_{p,2}+W^{s,\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(0,\lambda)\to\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\mathrm{sym}}(H^{s}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times(H^{s}_{p,2}+W^{s,\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{d});H^{s}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

via the formula

(5.49)

\Lambda^{(r)}(F,f)[(F_{1},f_{1}),\dots,(F_{r},f_{r})]=\Lambda(\partial_{d}^{r}F,f)\prod_{\ell=1}^{r}f_{\ell}+\sum_{m=1}^{r}\Lambda(\partial_{d}^{r-1}F_{m},f)\prod_{\ell\neq m}f_{\ell}.

In the above
\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\mathrm{sym}}
refers to the space of symmetric
r
-multilinear maps. By using the already established continuity properties of
\Lambda
along with Remark
5.11
, we see that
\Lambda^{(r)}
is continuous for
r\in\{{1,\dots,k}\}
. By similar considerations, we find that the map

(5.50)

\mathcal{R}_{k}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{L}^{k}_{\mathrm{sym}}(H^{s}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times(H^{s}_{p,2}+W^{s,\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{d});H^{s}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

defined via

(5.51)

\mathcal{R}_{k}((F,f),(G,g))=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(1-t)^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}(\Lambda^{(k)}(F+tG,f+tg)-\Lambda^{(k)}(F,f))\;\mathrm{d}t

is continuous, where

(5.52)

\mathcal{U}=\{{((F,f),(G,g))\in(H^{s+k}_{p,2}\times B(0,\lambda))^{2}\;:\;\forall\;t\in[0,1]\;(F+tG,f+tg)\in H^{s+k}_{p,2}\times B(0,\lambda)}\}.

Let
((F,f),(G,g))\in\mathcal{U}
. Now, according to Taylor’s theorem with integral remainder (used pointwise), we have that

(5.53)

\Lambda(F,f+g)-\Lambda(F,f)=\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{1}{r!}\Lambda(\partial_{d}^{r}F,f)g^{r}+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(1-t)^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}(\Lambda(\partial_{d}^{k}F,f+tg)-\Lambda(\partial_{d}^{k}F,f))g^{k}\;\mathrm{d}t.

We can also apply the same result to express for
h(s)=s\Lambda(G,f+sg)

(5.54)

\Lambda(G,f+g)=h(1)-h(0)=\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{1}{r!}(\partial^{r}h)(0)+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(1-t)^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}((\partial^{k}h)(t)-(\partial^{k}h)(0))\;\mathrm{d}t\\
=\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{1}{(r-1)!}\Lambda(\partial_{d}^{r-1}G,f)g^{r-1}\\
+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(1-t)^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}(t\Lambda(\partial_{d}^{k}G,f+tg)g^{k}+k(\Lambda(\partial_{d}^{k-1}G,f+tg)-\Lambda(\partial_{d}^{k-1}G,f))g^{k-1})\;\mathrm{d}t.

Adding (
5.53
) and (
5.54
) and using definitions (
5.49
) and (
5.51
) yields

(5.55)

\Lambda(F+G,f+g)-\Lambda(F,f)=\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{1}{r!}\Lambda^{(r)}(F,f)[(G,g)^{\otimes r}]+\mathcal{R}_{k}((F,f),(G,g))[(G,g)^{\otimes k}].

Therefore, by the converse to Taylor’s theorem, we deduce that
\Lambda
is
C^{k}
on its domain.
∎

5.3.
Subcritical gradient spaces

We now turn our attention to subcritical gradient spaces.

Definition 5.14

(Subcritical gradient spaces)
.

For
1<p<d
,
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, and
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
we define the space

(5.56)

\widetilde{H}^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})=\{{f\in L^{dp/(d-p)}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})\;:\;\nabla f\in H^{s-1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F}^{d})}\}

and it equip it with a norm
\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s,p}}=\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{H^{s-1,p}}
. When
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
we will typically abbreviate
\widetilde{H}^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\widetilde{H}^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
.

Next, we verify that these spaces are complete.

Proposition 5.15

(Properties of subcritical gradient spaces)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
1<p<d
, and
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
. Then the space
\widetilde{H}^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})
defined in (
5.56
) is Banach, and we have the bound

(5.57)

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{s-1,dp/(d-p)}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s,p}}\text{ for all }f\in\widetilde{H}^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F}).

Moreover,
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})\subset\widetilde{H}^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})
is dense.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the result when
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
, so we will assume this. We first consider the case
s=1
and note that in this case
\widetilde{H}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\dot{W}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{dp/(d-p)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for the homogeneous Sobolev space
\dot{W}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\{{f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\;:\;\nabla f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})}\}
. Next, we note that a density result of Hajłasz and Kałamajska
[
44
]
shows that if
f\in\dot{W}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then there exists a sequence
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\left\lVert\nabla f_{n}-\nabla f\right\rVert_{L^{p}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Additionally, Theorem 12.9 in Leoni
[
55
]
, which is crucially based on this density assertion, provides a constant
C_{p}>0
such that for each
f\in\dot{W}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
there exist a unique constant
c_{f}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\left\lVert f-c_{f}\right\rVert_{L^{dp/(d-p)}}\leqslant C_{p}\left\lVert\nabla f\right\rVert_{L^{p}}
. Now, if
f\in\widetilde{H}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
then upon applying this bound to
f
and noting that
f\in L^{dp/(d-p)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we deduce that
c_{f}=0
. Hence, (
5.57
) holds when
s=1
, and with this bound in hand it is a routine matter to verify that
\widetilde{H}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is complete. The above density assertion shows that
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R})
is dense in
\widetilde{H}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. To complete the proof for general
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
we simply note that the map
\langle D\rangle^{s-1}:\widetilde{H}^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\widetilde{H}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is an isometric isomorphism that maps
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R})
to itself.
∎

Next we record a frequency splitting result.

Lemma 5.16

(Frequency splitting in gradient spaces)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
1<p<d
, and
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
. Suppose that
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is an even function that satisfies
\varphi=1
on
B(0,1)
and
\supp(\varphi)\subseteq B(0,2)
. Then
(1-\varphi)(D):\widetilde{H}^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})\to H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})
is a bounded linear map, and
\varphi(D):\widetilde{H}^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})\to W^{k,dp/(d-p)}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})
is a bounded linear map for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward and we only sketch it. The mapping properties for
\varphi(D)
follow directly from the embedding
\widetilde{H}^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})\hookrightarrow L^{dp/(d-p)}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})
and that
\varphi(D)
is convolution with a band-limited Schwartz function. The mapping properties of
(1-\varphi(D))
are verified via the Littlewood-Paley characterizations from Theorems
3.10
and (
3.11
). The evenness of
\varphi
guarantees that real-valued maps stay real-valued when either
\varphi(D)
or
(1-\varphi)(D)
is applied.
∎

By using the lifting map of Proposition
5.5
, we can build a useful extension operator.

Proposition 5.17

(Extension operator)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
1<p<d
, and
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
. For
b\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
write
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,b)
and
\Sigma=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{b\}
. There exists a bounded linear operator
\mathcal{E}_{0}:\widetilde{H}^{3/2+s,p}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})\to H^{2+s}_{p,2}(\Omega;\mathbb{F})
and a linear operator
\mathcal{E}_{1}:\widetilde{H}^{3/2+s,p}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})\to W^{k,\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{F})
that is bounded for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that the linear extension operator
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{0}+\mathcal{E}_{1}
satisfies

(5.58)

\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}\mathcal{E}\eta=0\text{ and }\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}\mathcal{E}\eta=\eta\text{ for all }\eta\in\widetilde{H}^{3/2+s,p}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F}).

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be as in Lemma
5.16
. We define
\mathcal{E}_{0}
via

(5.59)

(\mathcal{E}_{0}\eta)(x,y)=\phi(b-y)(\mathrm{L}_{\Omega}(1-\varphi)(D)\eta)(x,y)\text{ for }(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,b),

where
\mathrm{L}_{\Omega}
is the lifting operator from Proposition
5.5
and
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies
\phi(0)=1
and
\supp(\phi)\subseteq(-b/2,b/2)
. We also define
\mathcal{E}_{1}
via

(5.60)

(\mathcal{E}_{1}\eta)(x,y)=(y/b)(\varphi(D)\eta)(x)\text{ for }(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,b).

The claimed mapping properties then follow immediately from Lemma
5.16
and Proposition
5.5
.
∎

6.
Linear analysis in mixed-type Sobolev spaces

In this penultimate section, we conclude our linear theory for systems (
2.2
) and (
2.1
). In Section
6.1
, we synthesize our multiplier analysis of Section
4
with our generalization of the Mikhlin-Hörmander, Theorem
3.14
, and produce a linear well-posedness result for (
2.2
) posed in the mixed-type Sobolev spaces of Section
5.1
. In Section
6.2
we then return to the main linear equations, system (
2.1
), and port over the extended linear theory for (
2.2
). This lands us a linear well-posedness theory for (
2.1
) that employs both the mixed-type Sobolev spaces of Section
5.1
and the subcritical gradient spaces of Section
5.3
. Armed with this result, we will then be ready to turn to the nonlinear analysis in Section
7
.

6.1.
Existence and uniqueness

We begin by setting some notation for the spaces in which we wish to extend our existence theory. Note that the mixed-type Sobolev spaces, which were introduced in Section
5.1
, are used here.

Definition 6.1

(Mixed-type function spaces, I)
.

For
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
1<r<\infty
, we define the function spaces

(6.1)

\mathbb{X}_{s,r}=H^{1+s}_{r,2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C})\times H^{2+s}_{r,2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})\times H^{3/2+s,r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{2}),

(6.2)

\mathbb{Y}_{s,r}=H^{s}_{r,2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})\times H^{1/2+s,r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{3})\times H^{5/2+s,r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{2}).

We are now ready to state and prove our main existence result of this subsection. We remark that
\mathbb{Y}_{s}\cap\mathbb{Y}_{s,r}
is dense in
\mathbb{Y}_{s,r}
. It is in this sense we use the word ‘extension’ in what follows.

Theorem 6.2

(Extension to mixed type spaces)
.

For
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
1<r<\infty
the linear map
\Psi:\mathbb{Y}_{s}\to\mathbb{X}_{s}
from Definition
4.1
has a unique bounded extension

(6.3)

\Psi:\mathbb{Y}_{s,r}\to\mathbb{X}_{s,r}.

Moreover, the above extension retains the property that if
(p,u,\chi)=\Psi(f,k,H)
, then system (
2.2
) is solved by the former tuple with data
(0,f,k,\nabla_{\|}\cdot H,0)
.

Proof.

\Psi
is given by the Fourier multiplier
\mathbf{m}
from Definition
4.6
, and
\mathbf{m}
satisfies Mikhlin-Hörmander bounds on its derivatives thanks to estimate (
4.48
) from Theorem
4.13
and the definition of
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{s}
from Definition
4.2
. We then apply our Mikhlin-Hörmander variant, Theorem
3.14
(with
\mu=0
), to each of the component maps
\mathbf{m}_{jk}
for
j,k\in\{1,2,3\}
to deduce the existence of the stated bounded extension. It is straightforward to check by density that these extensions of
\Psi
are still solution operators to (
2.2
).
∎

Our linear analysis for the system (
2.2
) is synthesized with the following theorem.

Theorem 6.3

(Well-posedness of the linearization in mixed-type Sobolev spaces, I)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
1<r\leqslant 2
. For every
(f,k,H)\in\mathbb{Y}_{s,r}
, there exists a unique
(p,u,\chi)\in\mathbb{X}_{s,r}
such that (
2.2
) is satisfied in the strong sense with data
(0,f,k,\nabla_{\|}\cdot H,0)
.

Proof.

Existence follows from Theorem
6.2
, so it remains to prove uniqueness. So suppose that
(p,u,\chi)\in\mathbb{X}_{s,r}
solve (
2.2
) with trivial data. Let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be such that
\varphi=1
in
B(0,1)
. Then for every
N
we have the inclusion
\varphi(D/N)(p,u,\chi)\in\mathbb{X}_{s}
thanks to Young’s inequality and the fact that
\varphi(D/N)
is a tangential convolution with a Schwartz function; moreover, the triple
\varphi(D/N)(p,u,\chi)
remains a solution to (
2.2
) with trivial data. We may thus invoke Theorem
2.6
to deduce that
\varphi(D/N)(p,u,\chi)=0
. As this holds for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we necessarily have that
(p,u,\chi)=0
. Uniqueness is proved.
∎

6.2.
Reformulated well-posedness

We now aim to make the transition from system (
2.2
) back to the original linearization (
2.1
). The previous subsection gave us the well-posedness of the former system in the mixed-type Sobolev spaces. The goal of this subsection is to port these result to the latter, and specialize to
\mathbb{R}
-valued functions. Note that the following definition implements the notions of subcritical gradient spaces, which were introduced in Section
5.3
.

Definition 6.4

(Mixed-type function spaces, II)
.

For
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
1<r<2
, we define the Banach spaces

(6.4)

\mathbf{X}_{s,r}=H^{1+s}_{r,2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\times H^{2+s}_{r,2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})\times\widetilde{H}^{5/2+s,r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}),

(6.5)

\mathbf{Y}_{s,r}=H^{s}_{r,2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{1/2+s,r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{3})\times(H^{3/2+s,r}\cap\dot{H}^{-1,r})(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}).

Note that the space
\dot{H}^{-1,r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
is defined in (
1.28
).

Provided that
s\in\mathbb{N}
is sufficiently large relative to
r\in(1,2)
, the spaces
\mathbf{X}_{s,r}
enjoy classical regularity.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
1<r<2
and
\mathbb{N}\ni s>3/r-1
. Then

(6.6)

\mathbf{X}_{s,r}\hookrightarrow C^{k}_{0}(\Omega)\times C^{1+k}_{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})\times C^{2+k}_{0}(\Sigma)

for
k=s-\lfloor 3/r\rfloor\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

The embedding of the first two factors follows from the first item of Proposition
5.8
. For the third factor, the embedding follows from Lemma
5.16
, the standard embedding of Bessel-Sobolev spaces, and the observation that
\lfloor 3/r\rfloor\geqslant 1+\lfloor 2/r-1/2\rfloor
for
1<r<2
.
∎

We can now state our well-posedness result.

Theorem 6.6

(Well-posedness of the linearization in mixed-type Sobolev spaces, II)
.

For every
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
1<r<2
, and
(f,k,h)\in\mathbf{Y}_{s,r}
there exists a unique
(p,u,\eta)\in\mathbf{X}_{s,r}
such that system (
2.1
) is solved with data
(0,f,k,h)
.

Proof.

We begin by proving uniqueness. Suppose that
(p,u,\eta)\in\mathbf{X}_{s,r}
solve system (
2.1
) with trivial data. Then we set
\chi=\nabla_{\|}\eta
and observe that
(p,u,\chi)\in\mathbb{X}_{s,r}
solves (
2.2
) with trivial data. Then Theorem
6.3
applies, and we learn that
(p,u,\chi)=0
and hence
\eta
is constant. However,
\eta\in L^{2r/(2-r)}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
, so
\eta=0
. This completes the proof of uniqueness.

We now prove existence. Suppose that
(f,k,h)\in\mathbf{Y}_{s,r}
. Using Mikhlin-Hörmander Theorem
3.8
, we may define
H=\nabla_{\|}\Delta_{\|}^{-1}h\in H^{5/2+s,r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{2})
, which obeys the estimate
\lVert H\rVert_{H^{5/2+s,r}}\lesssim\lVert h\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-1,r}\cap H^{3/2+s,r}}
as well as the identity
\nabla_{\|}\cdot H=h
. We may then use Theorem
6.3
to acquire
(p,u,\chi)=\Psi(f,k,H)\in\mathbb{X}_{s,r}
. Next, we define
\widetilde{\eta}=|\nabla_{\|}|\Delta_{\|}^{-1}\nabla_{\|}\cdot\chi
and note that
\widetilde{\eta}\in H^{3/2+s,r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C})
thanks to another application of Mikhlin-Hörmander. In turn, this allows us to employ the Hardy-Littlewood-Sobolev inequality (see, for instance, Theorem 1 in Section 1.2 in Chapter V of Stein
[
87
]
) to set
\eta=|\nabla_{\|}|^{-1}\widetilde{\eta}\in L^{2r/(2-r)}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C})
. Since
\nabla^{\perp}_{\|}\cdot\chi=0
, we then have that
\nabla_{\|}\eta=\chi\in H^{3/2+s,r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{2})
, and so
\eta\in\widetilde{H}^{5/2+s,r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C})
.

At this point we have established that
(p,u,\eta)
is a solution to (
2.1
) with the correct regularity and integrability properties, but the construction we have used does not guarantee a priori that the solution is real-valued. To see that this is actually the case, we note that since
(f,k,H)
have vanishing imaginary part, we can take the imaginary part of the equations and use the fact that its coefficients are all real to deduce that
(\mathrm{Im}p,\mathrm{Im}u,\mathrm{Im}\chi)=\Psi(0,0,0)
. Thus, Theorem
6.3
’s uniqueness assertion shows that
(\mathrm{Im}p,\mathrm{Im}u,\mathrm{Im}\chi)=0
, and the existence proof is complete.
∎

7.
Nonlinear analysis

In this section we complete the proof of our main result, Theorem
1
, by synthesizing our previous analysis and appealing to the implicit function theorem. Section
7.1
sets up the nonlinear framework, and then our main results are proved in Section
7.2
.

7.1.
Operators and mapping properties

The goal of this subsection is to define a nonlinear operator associated with system (
1.13
) and study its mapping properties. We begin by studying the flattening map
\eta\mapsto\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}
, which we recall is defined in (
1.10
).

Proposition 7.1

(Properties of the flattening map)
.

For
1<r<2
and
\mathbb{N}\ni 1+s>3/r
, there exists
\varrho\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that the following hold.

(1)

For
\eta\in B(0,\varrho)\subset\widetilde{H}^{3/2+s,r}(\Sigma)
the flattening map
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}+\mathcal{E}\eta e_{3}
is a smooth diffeomorphism from
\Omega
to
\Omega[\eta]
that extends to a
C^{n}
diffeomorphism from
\overline{\Omega}
to
\overline{\Omega[\eta]}
for
\mathbb{N}\ni n<s+2-3/r
.

(2)

Let
V
be a finite dimensional real normed space. For
\ell,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\leqslant 2+s
the map

(7.1)

H^{\ell+m}_{r,2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};V)\times B_{\widetilde{H}^{3/2+s,r}(\Sigma)}(0,\varrho)\ni(F,\eta)\mapsto F\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}\in H^{m}_{r,2}(\Omega;V)

is well-defined and
C^{\ell}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
denote the extension operator granted by Proposition
5.3
, and consider the extended flattening map
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{\eta}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
defined by
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{\eta}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}+\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\mathcal{E}\eta e_{3}
.
By the mapping properties of
\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
and
\mathcal{E}
from Propositions
5.3
and
5.17
, we find that the map
E
defined by

(7.2)

\widetilde{H}^{3/2+s,r}(\Sigma)\ni\eta\mapsto E\eta=\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\mathcal{E}\eta\in(H^{2+s}_{r,2}+W^{2+s,\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{3})

is bounded and linear. As such, there exists
\varrho\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that
E(B(0,\varrho))\subseteq B(0,\lambda)
, where
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
is the constant from Proposition
5.13
. We may then invoke the conclusions of this proposition to deduce the mapping properties asserted in (
7.1
). This completes the proof of the second item.

To prove the first item we only need to make a trio of observations. First, Proposition
5.13
shows that
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{\eta}
is a
C^{n}
diffeomorphism from
\mathbb{R}^{3}
to itself when
\mathbb{N}\ni n<s+2-3/r
. Second,
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}
is the restriction of
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{\eta}
to
\overline{\Omega}
, and by construction
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}
maps
\overline{\Omega}
to
\overline{\Omega[\eta]}
. Third, the construction of
\mathcal{E}\eta
shows that its restriction to
\Omega
itself is smooth. Together, these prove the first item.

∎

Next, we analyze quantities derived from the flattening map. Recall that
J_{\eta}
,
\mathcal{A}_{\eta}
, and
M_{\eta}
are defined in (
1.11
) and (
1.12
).

Proposition 7.2

(Properties of the Jacobian and geometry matrices)
.

Let
1<r<2
,
\mathbb{N}\ni 1+s>3/r
, and
\varrho\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
(depending on
s
and
r
) be as in Proposition
7.1
. Then the following mapping properties hold.

(1)

For
\eta\in\widetilde{H}^{3/2+s,r}(\Sigma)
, we have that
J_{\eta}>0
and both of the maps

(7.3)

\widetilde{H}^{3/2+s,r}(\Sigma)\supset B(0,\varrho)\ni\eta\mapsto J_{\eta},1/J_{\eta}\in(H^{1+s}_{r,2}+W^{1+s,\infty})(\Omega)

are smooth.

(2)

The maps

(7.4)

\widetilde{H}^{3/2+s,r}(\Sigma)\supset B(0,\varrho)\ni\eta\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{\eta},\mathcal{A}_{\eta}^{-1}\in(H^{1+s}_{r,2}+W^{1+s,\infty})(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})

are smooth.

(3)

The maps

(7.5)

\widetilde{H}^{3/2+s,r}(\Sigma)\supset B(0,\varrho)\ni\eta\mapsto M_{\eta},M_{\eta}^{-1}\in(H^{1+s}_{r,2}+W^{1+s,\infty})(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})

are smooth.

Proof.

The maps
\eta\mapsto J_{\eta}
and
\eta\mapsto M_{\eta}
are affine, and thus smooth thanks to Proposition
5.17
. By invoking Corollary
5.12
, we have that
\eta\mapsto 1/J_{\eta}
is smooth. This fact combined with Proposition
5.10
implies that
\eta\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{\eta}=M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}/J_{\eta}
is smooth. For the smoothness of the pointwise inversion in the third item, we appeal to Proposition
5.10
again and the adjugate formula
\eta\mapsto M_{\eta}^{-1}=\mathrm{adj}(M_{\eta})/J_{\eta}^{2}
. The remaining assertion, the smoothness of pointwise inversion in the second item is then handled via the formula
\eta\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{\eta}^{-1}=J_{\eta}M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}
.
∎

Now, working towards a synthesis, we make the following definitions. First we define some spaces.

Definition 7.3

(Spaces for the nonlinear analysis)
.

We make the following definitions for
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
1<r<2
, and
\rho\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
:

(1)

{{}_{\diamond}}H^{2+s}_{r,2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})=\{{u\in H^{2+s}_{r,2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})\;:\;\nabla\cdot u=0,\;\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}u=0}\}
,

(2)

{{}_{\diamond}}\mathbf{X}_{s,r}=H^{1+s}_{r,2}(\Omega)\times{{{}_{\diamond}}H^{2+s}_{r,2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\times\widetilde{H}^{5/2+s,r}(\Sigma)
,

(3)

\mathcal{O}_{s,r}(\rho)=\{{(p,u,\eta)\in{{{}_{\diamond}}\mathbf{X}_{s,r}}\;:\;\eta\in B(0,\rho)}\}
,

(4)

\mathbf{W}_{s,r}=H^{1+s}_{r,2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})\times H^{s}_{r,2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

Next, we define some maps.

Definition 7.4

(Maps for the nonlinear analysis, I)
.

For
1<r<2
,
\mathbb{N}\ni s>3/r
, and
\varrho\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
as in Proposition
7.1
we make the following definitions.

(1)

\Xi_{1}:\mathcal{O}_{s,r}(\varrho)\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\to H^{s}_{r,2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
is defined via

(7.6)

\Xi_{1}(p,u,\eta,\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu)=M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}((u-\gamma M_{\eta}e_{1})\cdot\nabla(M_{\eta}^{-1}u))+\nabla(p+\mathfrak{g}\eta)-\mu M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}(\nabla\cdot((\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u))M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}})).

(2)

\Xi_{2}:\mathcal{O}_{s,r}(\varrho)\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\to H^{1/2+s,r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{3})
is defined via

(7.7)

\Xi_{2}(p,u,\eta,\mu,\kappa)=\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}[-(pI-\mu\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u))M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{3}-\kappa\mathscr{H}(\eta)M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{3}].

(3)

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varrho
as in Proposition
7.1
,
\Upsilon_{1}:H^{m+s}_{r,2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})\times B_{\widetilde{H}^{3/2+s,r}}(0,\varrho)\to H^{s}_{r,2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
is defined via

(7.8)

\Upsilon_{1}(\mathcal{F},\eta)=-J_{\eta}M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}(\mathcal{F}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}).

(4)

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varrho
as in Proposition
7.1
,
\Upsilon_{2}:H^{m+1+s}_{r,2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})\times B_{\widetilde{H}^{3/2+s,r}}(0,\varrho)\to H^{1/2+s,r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{3})
is defined via

(7.9)

\Upsilon_{2}(\mathcal{T},\eta)=-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}[(\mathcal{T}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta})M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{3}].

Our next two results study the smoothness of the nonlinear differential operators in the momentum equation and dynamic boundary condition in system (
1.13
).

Proposition 7.5

(Mapping properties of the nonlinearities)
.

For
1<r<2
and
\mathbb{N}\ni s>3/r
, the following mapping properties hold.

(1)

\Xi_{1}
and
\Xi_{2}
, as defined in the first and second items of Definition
7.4
, are smooth.

(2)

\Upsilon_{1}
and
\Upsilon_{2}
, as defined in the third and fourth items of Definition
7.4
are
C^{m}
.

Proof.

The first item follows from Propositions
7.2
and
5.9
along with Remark
5.11
, since all of the nonlinearities in
\Xi_{1}
are various products of the derivatives of the velocity field with the geometry matrices and parameters.

The analysis of
\Xi_{2}
follows similarly, with the exception of the mean curvature term
\mathscr{H}(\eta)
. For this we use that
\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni x\mapsto x\langle x\rangle^{-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is everywhere analytic and vanishing at zero and hence the map

(7.10)

H^{3/2+s,r}(\Sigma)^{2}\ni(\partial_{1}\eta,\partial_{2}\eta)\mapsto\langle\nabla_{\|}\eta\rangle^{-1}(\partial_{1}\eta,\partial_{2}\eta)\in H^{3/2+s,r}(\Sigma)^{2}

is smooth since
H^{3/2+s,r}(\Sigma)
is an algebra. In turn, we find that the map
\eta\mapsto\mathscr{H}(\eta)
is also smooth. This completes the proof of the first item.

The second item follow by similar considerations, supplemented with the second item of Proposition
7.1
.
∎

The remainder of this subsection’s nonlinear analysis is meant to deal with the technicalities arising in the slowly traveling limit (
\gamma\to 0
) in system (
1.13
). As the equations are currently formulated, there is a change of natural function spaces that occurs in this limit, which suggests that the stationary problem is a (low-mode) singular limit of traveling problems. The effect of this is that the formulation (
1.13
) works fine for
\gamma=0
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{{0}\}
separately, but is ill-suited for capturing the slowly traveling limit
\gamma\to 0
. To overcome this issue, we make a change of unknowns in the free surface.

Definition 7.6

(Anisotropic parameterization operators)
.

For
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
we let
P_{\gamma}
be the Fourier multiplication operator with the following symbol

(7.11)

P_{\gamma}=\mathbf{p}_{\gamma}(D),\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{p}_{\gamma}(\xi)=\frac{4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}}{4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}+2\pi\mathrm{i}\gamma\xi_{1}},

Note that
P_{0}
is the identity operator.

The relevant properties of the maps
P_{\gamma}
are enumerated in the following result. Recall that the spaces
\dot{H}^{-1,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
are defined in (
1.28
).

Proposition 7.7

(Properties of the anisotropic parameterization operators)
.

The following hold for
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
1<r<2
.

(1)

For each
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
we have that
P_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{H}^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}))
. Moreover, the map
\mathbb{R}\ni\gamma\mapsto P_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{H}^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}))
is bounded, and for any
\eta\in\widetilde{H}^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
the map
\mathbb{R}\ni\gamma\mapsto P_{\gamma}\eta\in\widetilde{H}^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
is continuous.

(2)

For each
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
we have that
\gamma\partial_{1}P_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{H}^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R});\dot{H}^{-1,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}))
. Moreover, the map
\mathbb{R}\ni\gamma\mapsto\gamma\partial_{1}P_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{H}^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}))
is bounded, and for any
\eta\in\widetilde{H}^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
the map
\mathbb{R}\ni\gamma\mapsto\gamma\partial_{1}P_{\gamma}\eta\in\dot{H}^{-1,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
is continuous.

(3)

The mappings

(7.12)

\mathbb{R}\times\widetilde{H}^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})\ni(\gamma,\upeta)\mapsto\begin{cases}P_{\gamma}\upeta\in\widetilde{H}^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}),\\
\gamma\partial_{1}P_{\gamma}\upeta\in\dot{H}^{-1,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}),\\
\gamma\mathcal{R}_{1}P_{\gamma}\upeta\in H^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})\end{cases}

are continuous, where
\mathcal{R}_{1}
refers to the Riesz transform in the
e_{1}
direction.

Proof.

The third item follows from the first two, so we turn our attention to proving these.

We claim that the multiplier
\mathbf{p}_{\gamma}(\xi)=\frac{4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}}{4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}+2\pi\mathrm{i}\gamma\xi_{1}}
is of Marcinkiewicz type (see Theorem
3.7
) and the defining inequalities (
3.18
) are satisfied uniformly over
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
. To prove the claim, we first note that

(7.13)

|\mathbf{p}_{\gamma}(\xi)|\leqslant 1

and that
\mathbf{p}_{\gamma}
is smooth away from the coordinate axes. Next, we compute

(7.14)

\partial_{1}\mathbf{p}_{\gamma}(\xi)=\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}\gamma(\xi_{1}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2})}{(2\pi|\xi|^{2}+\mathrm{i}\gamma\xi_{1})^{2}},\hskip 10.00002pt\partial_{2}\mathbf{p}_{\gamma}(\xi)=\frac{4\pi\mathrm{i}\gamma\xi_{1}\xi_{2}}{(2\pi|\xi|^{2}+\mathrm{i}\gamma\xi_{1})^{2}},

and

(7.15)

\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\mathbf{p}_{\gamma}(\xi)=-\frac{4\pi\mathrm{i}\gamma\xi_{2}(6\pi\xi_{1}^{2}-2\pi\xi_{2}^{2}+\mathrm{i}\gamma\xi_{1})}{(2\pi|\xi|^{2}+\mathrm{i}\gamma\xi_{1})^{3}}.

Thus, we have the following estimates from Cauchy’s inequality:

(7.16)

|\xi_{1}\partial_{1}\mathbf{p}_{\gamma}(\xi)|\leqslant\frac{|\gamma\xi_{1}|2\pi|\xi|^{2}}{4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{4}+\gamma^{2}|\xi_{1}|^{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2},\hskip 10.00002pt|\xi_{2}\partial_{2}\mathbf{p}_{\gamma}(\xi)|\leqslant\frac{4\pi|\gamma\xi_{1}||\xi|^{2}}{4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{4}+\gamma^{2}|\xi_{1}|^{2}}\leqslant 1,

and

(7.17)

|\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\mathbf{p}_{\gamma}(\xi)|\leqslant\frac{12\pi|\gamma\xi_{1}||\xi|^{2}|2\pi|\xi|^{2}+\mathrm{i}\gamma\xi_{1}|}{(4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{4}+\gamma^{2}|\xi_{1}|^{2})^{3/2}}\leqslant\frac{12\pi|\gamma\xi_{1}||\xi|^{2}}{4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{4}+\gamma^{2}|\xi_{1}|^{2}}\leqslant 3.

This completes the proof of the claim, and so we may invoke Theorem
3.7
(with the observation that
\mathbf{p}_{\gamma}(-\xi)=\overline{\mathbf{p}_{\gamma}(\xi)}
for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{{0}\}
) to see that

(7.18)

\left\lVert P_{\gamma}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}\leqslant C_{r}(3+1)=4C_{r}

for a constant
C_{r}
depending only on
r
.

Armed with (
7.18
), we are now ready to prove the first item. If
\upeta\in\widetilde{H}^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
, then we have

(7.19)

\sup_{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}}\lVert P_{\gamma}\upeta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s,r}}=\sup_{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}}\lVert\mathbf{p}_{\gamma}(D)\langle D\rangle^{s-1}\nabla_{\|}\upeta\rVert_{L^{r}}\lesssim\lVert\langle D\rangle^{s-1}\nabla_{\|}\upeta\rVert_{L^{r}}\lesssim\lVert\upeta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s,r}}.

Next, we prove that
\gamma\mapsto P_{\gamma}
is continuous for the strong operator topology. Fix
\upeta\in\widetilde{H}^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Thanks to density of
H^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow\widetilde{H}^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
(see Proposition
5.15
), there exists
\upzeta\in H^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
such that
\lVert\upzeta-\upeta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s,r}}\leqslant\varepsilon
. Thus, we learn from (
7.18
) that if
\gamma,\gamma_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
then

(7.20)

\lVert(P_{\gamma}-P_{\gamma_{0}})\upeta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s,r}}\lesssim\varepsilon+\lVert(P_{\gamma}-P_{\gamma_{0}})\upzeta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s,r}}.

We compute via the fundamental theorem of calculus that

(7.21)

\mathbf{p}_{\gamma}(\xi)-\mathbf{p}_{\gamma_{0}}(\xi)=(\gamma-\gamma_{0})\frac{\mathrm{i}\xi_{1}}{2\pi|\xi|^{2}}\mathbf{q}_{\gamma,\gamma_{0}}(\xi)\text{ for }\mathbf{q}_{\gamma,\gamma_{0}}(\xi)=\int_{0}^{1}(\mathbf{p}_{t\gamma+(1-t)\gamma_{0}}(\xi))^{2}\;\mathrm{d}t.

Estimates (
7.13
), (
7.16
), and (
7.17
) and the Leibniz rule show that

(7.22)

\sup_{\gamma,\gamma_{0}}\left(|\mathbf{q}_{\gamma,\gamma_{0}}(\xi)|+|\xi_{1}\partial_{1}\mathbf{q}_{\gamma,\gamma_{0}}(\xi)|+|\xi_{2}\partial_{2}\mathbf{q}_{\gamma,\gamma_{0}}(\xi)|+|\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\mathbf{q}_{\gamma,\gamma_{0}}(\xi)|\right)\lesssim 1,

and we also have that
\mathbf{q}_{\gamma,\gamma_{0}}(-\xi)=\overline{\mathbf{q}_{\gamma,\gamma_{0}}(\xi)}
, so we may once more appeal to Theorem
3.7
to learn that

(7.23)

\sup_{\gamma,\gamma_{0}}\left\lVert\mathbf{q}_{\gamma,\gamma_{0}}(D)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}\lesssim 1.

Writing
\mathcal{R}=(\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2})
for the vector of Riesz transforms, we then have that

(7.24)

\lVert(P_{\gamma}-P_{\gamma_{0}})\upzeta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s,r}}=\lVert\nabla(P_{\gamma}-P_{\gamma_{0}})\upzeta\rVert_{H^{s-1,r}}=\frac{|\gamma-\gamma_{0}|}{2\pi}\lVert\mathbf{q}_{\gamma,\gamma_{0}}(D)\mathcal{R}\mathcal{R}_{1}\left\langle D\right\rangle^{s-1}\upzeta\rVert_{L^{r}}\\
\lesssim|\gamma-\gamma_{0}|\lVert\left\langle D\right\rangle^{s-1}\zeta\rVert_{L^{r}}=|\gamma-\gamma_{0}|\lVert\zeta\rVert_{H^{s-1,r}}.

By combining (
7.20
) and (
7.24
), we get

(7.25)

\limsup_{\gamma_{0}\to\gamma}\lVert(P_{\gamma}-P_{\gamma_{0}})\upeta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s,r}}\lesssim\varepsilon,

so the continuity claim follows. This completes the proof of the first item.

The second item is proved by similar considerations thanks to the identity

(7.26)

\upgamma\mathcal{R}_{1}P_{\upgamma}=(1-P_{\upgamma})2\pi|D|=(P_{\upgamma}-1)\mathcal{R}\cdot\nabla,

which shows that for
\upeta\in\widetilde{H}^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})

(7.27)

\sup_{\gamma}[{\gamma\partial_{1}P_{\gamma}\upeta}]_{\dot{H}^{-1,r}}=\sup_{\gamma}\lVert\gamma\mathcal{R}_{1}P_{\gamma}\upeta\rVert_{L^{r}}=\sup_{\gamma}\lVert(P_{\gamma}-1)\mathcal{R}\cdot\nabla\upeta\rVert_{L^{r}}\lesssim\lVert\eta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s,r}},

where the last inequality follows from the (
7.18
) and the
L^{r}
boundedness of Riesz transforms. For continuity, we again fix
\eta\in\widetilde{H}^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
,
\gamma_{0},\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
and then use (
7.26
) to deduce that

(7.28)

[{(\gamma\partial_{1}P_{\gamma}-\gamma_{0}\partial_{1}P_{\gamma_{0}})\upeta}]_{\dot{H}^{-1,r}}\lesssim\lVert(P_{\gamma_{0}}-P_{\gamma})\mathcal{R}\cdot\nabla\upeta\rVert_{L^{r}}\lesssim\lVert(P_{\gamma_{0}}-P_{\gamma})\upeta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s,r}},

which means that the continuity assertion of the second item follows from that of the first. This completes the proof of the second item.
∎

The operators of Definition
7.6
permit us to make a change of unknowns in (
1.13
) to overcome the aforementioned singular limit issue. We consider
\eta=P_{\gamma}\upeta
, and view
\upeta
as our new unknown. The main theorem of this section’s nonlinear analysis, which implements this change of unknowns crucially, is now given as follows.

Theorem 7.8

(Mapping properties)
.

For
1<r<2
,
\mathbb{N}\ni s>3/r
, and
\varrho\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
as in Proposition
7.1
, there exists
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that the map
\Upxi:\mathcal{O}_{s,r}(\varrho/C)\times\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times\mathbf{W}_{1+s,r}\to\mathbf{Y}_{s,r}
given by

(7.29)

\Upxi(p,u,\upeta,\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\\
=(\Xi_{1}(p,u,P_{\gamma}\upeta,\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu)+\Upsilon_{1}(\mathcal{F},P_{\gamma}\upeta),\Xi_{2}(p,u,P_{\gamma}\upeta,\mu,\kappa)+\Upsilon_{2}(\mathcal{T},P_{\gamma}\upeta),\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}u\cdot e_{3}+\gamma\partial_{1}P_{\gamma}\upeta)

is well-defined and continuous. Moreover, the Fréchet derivative with respect to the first factor,
D_{1}\Upxi:\mathcal{O}_{s,r}(\varrho/C)\times\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times\mathbf{W}_{1+s,r}\to\mathcal{L}({{}_{\diamond}}\mathbf{X}_{s,r};\mathbf{Y}_{s,r})
, exists and is continuous.

Proof.

The uniform boundedness assertions in the first item of Proposition
7.7
guarantee that for some
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
we have
(p,u,P_{\gamma}\upeta)\in\mathcal{O}_{s,r}(\varrho)
for all
(p,u,\upeta)\in\mathcal{O}_{s,r}(\varrho/C)
. Thus, by composition, linearity of
P_{\gamma}
, and the third item of Proposition
7.7
we may invoke is Proposition
7.5
to reach the desired conclusions for the first and second components of the map
\Upxi.
The third component of
\Upxi
is handled via the linearity of
\gamma\partial_{1}P_{\gamma}
, together with the second and third items of Proposition
7.7
and the divergence compatibility estimate of Proposition
5.6
.
∎

7.2.
Well-posedness

We are now ready to prove our main theorem. This subsection is split into four main results and then a list of corollaries, which combine to prove Theorem
1
. In the first main result, we invoke the implicit function theorem at a fixed tuple of positive physical parameters and obtain a solution map. In the next, we show that we can glue these together across all parameter values. One slight issue that remains after this is done is that the resulting solution map loses a derivative relative to what one would expect. This fact stems from the numerology of higher order smoothness of composition-type nonlinearities (see e.g. Proposition
5.13
). We are thus lead to the third result in this subsection, in which we show a posteriori that the solution map actually obeys the optimal derivative counting. In the fourth, and final, main result of this subsection, we recast the previous results into a more physically relevant formulation by anisotropically parameterizing the free surface variable with the operators in Proposition
7.7
.

We shall use the following version of the inverse function theorem, as formulated as in Theorem A in Crandall and Rabinowitz
[
30
]
(for a verbose proof see Theorem 2.7.2 in Nirenberg
[
61
]
, but note that there is slight misstatement of the uniqueness assertion in the first item there that is correct in
[
30
]
).

Theorem 7.9

(Implicit function theorem)
.

Let
X,Y,Z
be Banach spaces and
f
a continuous mapping of an open set
U\subset X\times Y\to Z
. Assume that
f
has a Fréchet derivative with respect to the first factor,
D_{1}f:U\to\mathcal{L}(X;Z)
that is continuous. Suppose that
(x_{0},y_{0})\in U
and
f(x_{0},y_{0})=0
. If
D_{1}f(x_{0},y_{0})
is an isomorphism of
X
onto
Z
, then there exist balls
B(y_{0},r_{Y})\subset Y
and
B(x_{0},r_{X})\subset X
such that
B(x_{0},r_{X})\times B(y_{0},r_{Y})\subset U
and a continuous unique function
u:B(y_{0},r_{Y})\to B(x_{0},r_{X})
such that
u(y_{0})=x_{0}
and
f(u(y),y)=0
for all
y\in B(y_{0},r_{Y})
. Moreover, the implicit function
u
is continuous.

We now apply Theorem
7.9
in our first well-posedness result.

Theorem 7.10

(Well-posedness, I)
.

Let
1<r<2
,
\mathbb{N}\ni s>3/r
, and
\varrho,C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
be as in Theorem
7.8
. For each
\mathbf{v}=(\overline{\mathfrak{g}},\overline{\mu},\overline{\kappa})\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}
there exists
\uprho_{s,\mathbf{v}},\uprho_{s,\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and a unique mapping

(7.30)

\upiota_{\mathbf{v}}:B((0,\mathbf{v},0),\uprho_{s,\mathbf{v}})\subset\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times\mathbf{W}_{1+s,r}\times\mathbf{Y}_{s,r}\to B(0,\uprho_{s,\mathbf{v}}^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{O}_{s,r}(\varrho/C)

with the property that for all data
(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F},f,k,h)\in B((0,\mathbf{v},0),\uprho_{s,\mathbf{v}})
we have that
(p,u,\upeta)=\upiota_{\mathbf{v}}(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F},f,k,h)\in\mathcal{O}_{s,r}(\varrho/C)
is a solution to

(7.31)

\begin{cases}M_{P_{\gamma}\upeta}^{-\mathrm{t}}((u-\gamma M_{P_{\gamma}\upeta}e_{1})\cdot\nabla(M_{P_{\gamma}\upeta}^{-1}u))+\nabla(p+\mathfrak{g}P_{\gamma}\upeta)\\
\quad-\mu M_{P_{\gamma}\upeta}^{-\mathrm{t}}(\nabla\cdot((\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}_{P_{\gamma}\upeta}}(M_{P_{\gamma}\upeta}^{-1}u))M_{P_{\gamma}\upeta}^{\mathrm{t}}))=f+J_{P_{\gamma}\upeta}M_{P_{\gamma}\upeta}^{-\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{F}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{P_{\gamma}\upeta}&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\nabla\cdot u=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\
-(p-\mu\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}_{P_{\gamma}\upeta}}(M_{P_{\gamma}\upeta}^{-1}u))M_{P_{\gamma}\upeta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{3}-\kappa\mathscr{H}(P_{\gamma}\upeta)M_{P_{\gamma}\upeta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{3}=k+\mathcal{T}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{P_{\gamma}\upeta}M_{P_{\gamma}\upeta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{3}&\text{on }\Sigma,\\
u\cdot e_{3}+\gamma\partial_{1}P_{\gamma}\upeta=h&\text{on }\Sigma,\\
u=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

Moreover,
\upiota_{\mathbf{v}}
in (
7.30
) is continuous.

Proof.

Consider the map
\overline{\Upxi}:\mathcal{O}_{s,r}(\varrho/C)\times\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times\mathbf{W}_{1+s,r}\times\mathbf{Y}_{s,r}\to\mathbf{Y}_{s,r}
defined via

(7.32)

\overline{\Upxi}(p,u,\upeta,\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F},f,k,h)=\Upxi(p,u,\upeta,\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})-(f,k,h).

Thanks to Theorem
7.8
, this map is well-defined, continuous, and the Fréchet derivative with respect to the first factor,
D_{1}\overline{\Upxi}:\mathcal{O}_{s,r}(\varrho/C)\times\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times\mathbf{W}_{1+s,r}\times\mathbf{Y}_{s,r}\to\mathcal{L}({{}_{\diamond}}\mathbf{X}_{s,r};\mathbf{Y}_{s,r})
, exists and is continuous. Moreover, we have that
\overline{\Xi}(0,\mathbf{v},0)=0
. Theorem
6.6
then shows that
D_{1}\overline{\Upxi}(0,\mathbf{v},0)
is an isomorphism from
{{}_{\diamond}}\mathbf{X}_{s,r}
to
\mathbf{Y}_{s,r}
. We may then invoke the version of the implicit function theorem given in Theorem
7.9
to obtain parameters
\uprho_{s,\mathbf{v}},\uprho_{s,\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
along with the map
\upiota_{\mathbf{v}}
.
∎

We can recast the previous theorem into the following more general statement via a gluing argument.

Theorem 7.11

(Well-posedness, II)
.

Let
1<r<2
,
\mathbb{N}\ni s>3/r
. There exists and open set

(7.33)

\{{0}\}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times\{{0}\}\times\{{0}\}\subset\mathscr{U}_{s}\subset\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times\mathbf{W}_{1+s,r}\times\mathbf{Y}_{s,r}

and a continuous map

(7.34)

\upiota:\mathscr{U}_{s}\to\bigcup_{\mathbf{v}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}}B(0,\uprho^{\prime}_{s,\mathbf{v}})\subset\mathcal{O}_{s,r}(\varrho/C),

where the radii
\uprho^{\prime}_{s,\mathbf{v}}>0
are as in Theorem
7.10
, with the property that for all
\mathbf{U}=(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F},f,k,h)\in\mathscr{U}_{s}
we have that
\upiota(\mathbf{U})=(p,u,\eta)\in B(0,\uprho^{\prime}_{s,(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)})
is the unique solution to (
7.31
) with data
\mathbf{U}
.

Proof.

We set

(7.35)

\mathscr{U}_{s}=\bigcup_{\mathbf{v}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}}B((0,\mathbf{v},0),\uprho_{s,\mathbf{v}}),

where
\uprho_{s,\mathbf{v}}
are the radii granted by Theorem
7.10
. We propose defining the map (
7.34
) via

(7.36)

\upiota=\upiota_{\mathbf{v}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on the set }B((0,\mathbf{v},0),\uprho_{s,\mathbf{v}})\text{ whenever }\mathbf{v}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3},

where the maps
\upiota_{\mathbf{v}}
are the solution operators granted by Theorem
7.10
. This is well-defined since if
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}
are such that
B((0,\mathbf{v},0),\uprho_{s,\mathbf{v}})\cap B((0,\mathbf{w},0),\uprho_{s,\mathbf{w}})\neq\varnothing
, then the maps
\upiota_{\mathbf{v}}
and
\upiota_{\mathbf{w}}
agree on this intersection since, according to the aforementioned theorem, they are both the unique solution operators to the PDE (
7.31
). Once we know that
\upiota
is well-defined, continuity follows from the continuity of each
\upiota_{\mathbf{v}}
. The remaining uniqueness assertions are just a restatement of those from Theorem
7.10
.
∎

The next well-posedness result gains an extra derivative on the solution to reach the optimal counting.

Theorem 7.12

(Well-posedness, III)
.

Let
1<r<2
,
\mathbb{N}\ni s>3/r+1
. There exists an open set

(7.37)

\{{0}\}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times\{{0}\}\subset\mathscr{W}_{s}\subset\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times\mathbf{W}_{s,r}

such that
\mathscr{W}_{s}\times\{{0}\}\subset\mathscr{U}_{s-1}
, where
\mathscr{U}_{s-1}
is as in Theorem
7.11
, and a continuous map

(7.38)

\widetilde{\upiota}:\mathscr{W}_{s}\to\mathbf{X}_{s,r}\cap\bigcup_{\mathbf{v}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}}B_{\mathbf{X}_{s-1,r}}(0,\uprho^{\prime}_{s-1,\mathbf{v}})\subset\mathcal{O}_{s,r}(\varrho/C)

with the property that for all
\mathbf{W}=(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{\mu},\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\in\mathscr{W}_{s}
we have that
\widetilde{\upiota}(\mathbf{W})=(p,u,\eta)\in\mathbf{X}_{s,r}\cap B_{\mathbf{X}_{s,r}}(0,\uprho^{\prime}_{s-1,(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)})
is the unique solution to (
7.31
) with data
(\mathbf{W},0)
.

Proof.

We set
\widetilde{\upiota}(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})=\upiota(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F},0,0,0)
, where
\upiota
is the solution operator granted by Theorem
7.11
. A priori, we only know that

(7.39)

\widetilde{\upiota}:\bigcup_{\mathbf{v}\in(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{+}}B((0,\mathbf{v},0),\uprho_{s,\mathbf{v}})\subset\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times\mathbf{W}_{s,r}\to\bigcup_{\mathbf{v}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}}B_{\mathbf{X}_{s-1,r}}(0,\uprho_{s-1,\mathbf{v}}^{\prime})

is a continuous mapping.

To complete the proof, we claim that by shrinking the domain a little bit, if necessary, we can gain an additional derivative on the solution. To see this let
0<\sigma\leqslant 1
and set

(7.40)

\mathscr{W}_{s}(\sigma)=\bigcup_{\mathbf{v}\in(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{+}}B((0,\mathbf{v},0),\sigma\uprho_{s,\mathbf{v}})\subset\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times\mathbf{W}_{s,r}.

Denote
(p,u,\upeta)=\widetilde{\upiota}(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
. By the second item of Proposition
7.5
, the continuity of the solution map in (
7.39
), and the continuity of composition we have that the map

(7.41)

\mathscr{W}_{s}(\sigma)\ni(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\mapsto(\Upupsilon_{1}(\mathcal{F},P_{\gamma}\upeta),\Upupsilon_{2}(\mathcal{T},P_{\gamma}\upeta),0)\in\mathbf{Y}_{s,r}

is continuous, vanishes whenever
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{F}
are zero, and is independent of
(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)
. Thus, by taking
0<\sigma_{\star}\leqslant 1
sufficiently small, we guarantee that

(7.42)

\mathscr{W}_{s}(\sigma_{\star})\ni(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\mapsto(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,0,0,\Upupsilon_{1}(\mathcal{F},P_{\gamma}\upeta),\Upupsilon_{2}(\mathcal{T},P_{\gamma}\upeta),0)\in\mathscr{U}_{s}.

Since the map in (
7.42
) actually has its range in the domain of the map
\upiota
, which is given in (
7.34
), we have verified the following key identity:

(7.43)

(p,u,\upeta)=\upiota(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,0,0,\Upupsilon_{1}(\mathcal{F},P_{\gamma}\upeta),\Upupsilon_{2}(\mathcal{T},P_{\gamma}\upeta),0).

Then the mapping properties of
\upiota
reveal that the solution

(7.44)

(p,u,\upeta)\in B_{\mathbf{X}_{s,r}}(0,\uprho^{\prime}_{s,(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)})\cap B_{\mathbf{X}_{s-1,r}}(0,\uprho^{\prime}_{s-1,(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)})

varies continuously with the data
(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\in\mathscr{W}_{s}=\mathscr{W}_{s}(\sigma_{\star})
. This proves the claim.
∎

This section’s list of main theorems is concluded with the following.

Theorem 7.13

(Well-posedness, IV)
.

Let
1<r<2
,
\mathbb{N}\ni s>3/r+1
, and
\mathscr{W}_{s}
be the open set from Theorem
7.12
. There exists a map

(7.45)

\mathscr{W}_{s}\ni(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\mapsto(p,u,\eta)\in\mathcal{O}_{s,r}(\varrho)

such that the following hold.

(1)

The map is a continuous solution operator to the nonlinear system (
1.13
).

(2)

The map is locally unique in the sense that there exist open sets
W_{s}\subseteq\mathscr{W}_{s}
and
\{{V_{s}(\mathbf{v})}\}_{\mathbf{v}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{s,r}(\varrho)
, obeying the non-degeneracy conditions of (
1.14
), for which the following two conditions hold.

(i)

The image of
W_{s}
under (
7.45
) is contained within
\bigcup_{\mathbf{v}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}}V_{s}(\mathbf{v})
.

(ii)

For each
\mathbf{v}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}
the restriction of (
7.45
) to the preimage of
V_{s}(\mathbf{v})
, thought of as a mapping to
V_{s}(\mathbf{v})
, is the unique function that is a solution operator to (
1.13
).

(3)

We have an extra ‘anisotropic’ estimate on the free surface in the sense that the composition map

(7.46)

\mathscr{W}_{s}\ni(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\mapsto(p,u,\eta)\mapsto\gamma\mathcal{R}_{1}\eta\in L^{r}(\Sigma)

is well-defined and continuous, where
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is the Riesz transform in the
e_{1}
direction.

Proof.

We take (
7.45
) to be the composition
(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\overset{\widetilde{\upiota}}{\mapsto}(p,u,\upeta)\mapsto(p,u,\eta)
, with
\eta=P_{\gamma}\upeta
. Thanks to Theorem
7.12
and Proposition
7.7
, this is a continuous solution operator for (
1.13
). The final item of the aforementioned proposition also guarantees that the mapping of (
7.46
) is well-defined and continuous.

It remains to establish local uniqueness. Suppose that
(p,u,\eta),(p^{\prime},u^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\in\mathcal{O}_{s,p}(\varrho)
are such that there exists
(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\in\mathscr{W}_{s}
and both
(p,u,\eta)
and
(p^{\prime},u^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
are solutions to (
1.13
) with the same data
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
, same wave speed
\gamma
, and same physical constants
(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)
. Integrating the divergence free constraint over
y\in(0,b)
and appealing to the kinematic boundary condition and the no slip condition yields the identity

(7.47)

\gamma\partial_{1}\zeta=(\nabla_{\|},0)\cdot\int_{0}^{b}w(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\text{ for }(\zeta,w)\in\{{(\eta,u),(\eta^{\prime},u^{\prime})}\}.

Therefore, by (
7.47
) and the identity
P_{\gamma}^{-1}\nabla=\nabla+\mathcal{R}\gamma\mathcal{R}_{1}
, where
\mathcal{R}=(\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2})
is the vector of Riesz transforms, we have the estimate

(7.48)

\lVert P_{\gamma}^{-1}\zeta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{5/2+s,r}}\lesssim\lVert\zeta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{5/2+s,r}}+\Big\lVert|D|^{-1}(\nabla_{\|},0)\cdot\int_{0}^{b}w(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big\rVert_{H^{3/2+s,r}}\lesssim\lVert w,\zeta\rVert_{H^{2+s}_{r,2}\times\widetilde{H}^{5/2+s,r}},

where in both of the inequalities we have applied the boundedness of Riesz transforms. If
(p,u,\eta)
and
(p^{\prime},u^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
are sufficiently small, we therefore guarantee that
(p,u,P_{\gamma}^{-1}\eta),(p^{\prime},u^{\prime},P_{\gamma}^{-1}\eta^{\prime})\in\mathbf{X}_{s,r}\cap B_{\mathbf{X}_{s-1,r}}(0,\uprho^{\prime}_{s-1,(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)})
, but we can then invoke the uniqueness assertion of Theorem
7.12
to find that
(p,u,P_{\gamma}^{-1}\eta)=(p^{\prime},u^{\prime},P_{\gamma}^{-1}\eta^{\prime})
, which implies
(p,u,\eta)=(p^{\prime},u^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
.

Thus, we take
V_{s}(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)
to be an open ball about the origin of a positive radius that obeys the above smallness requirements. The set
W_{s}
is then defined to be the union of the preimages of these balls under the map (
7.45
).
∎

We now enumerate some important consequences.

Corollary 7.14

(Some further conclusions)
.

For
1<r<2
and
\mathbb{N}\ni s>3/r+1
the following hold.

(1)

Classical solutions:
Each triple
(p,u,\eta)
produced by Theorem
7.13
is a classical solution
to system (
1.13
). More precisely, whenever
(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\in\mathscr{W}_{s}
we have that the associated solution satisfies

(7.49)

(p,u,\eta)\in C^{2+k}_{0}(\Omega)\times C^{3+k}_{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})\times C^{4+k}_{0}(\Sigma),

for
k=s-2-\lfloor 3/r\rfloor\in\mathbb{N}
.

(2)

Eulerian transfer:
Each solution
(p,u,\eta)
to system (
1.13
), produced by Theorem
7.13
gives rise to a corresponding classical solution

(7.50)

(q,v,\eta)\in C^{2+k}_{0}(\Omega[\eta])\times C^{3+k}_{0}(\Omega[\eta])\times C^{4+k}_{0}(\Omega[\eta]),\hskip 10.00002ptk=s-2-\lfloor 3/r\rfloor

to the stationary-traveling Eulerian formulation of the problem given by system (
1.8
) via unflattening.

(3)

Fixed physical parameters, variable wave speed:
For each
(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}
, there exists an open set
(0,0,0)\in W_{s}(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbf{W}_{s,r}
and a unique function

(7.51)

W_{s}(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)\ni(\gamma,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\mapsto(p,u,\eta)\in V_{s}(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)

with the property that for all
(\gamma,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
belonging to the domain, the corresponding
(p,u,\eta)
solves (
1.13
) with wave speed
\gamma
, physical parameters
(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)
, and stress-force data
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
. Moreover, the map (
7.51
) is continuous.

(4)

Well-posedness of the stationary wave problem:
There exists an open set
Z_{s}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times\mathbf{W}_{s,r}
satisfying (
1.17
) and continuous a map

(7.52)

Z_{s}\ni(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)\mapsto(p,u,\eta)\in\bigcup_{\mathbf{v}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}}V_{s}(\mathbf{v})

with the property that for all
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)\in Z_{s}
, the corresponding
(p,u,\eta)
belongs to the set
V_{s}(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)
and is the unique solution to (
1.13
) with
\gamma=0
in this set with data in
Z_{s}
.

Proof.

The first item follows from Proposition
6.5
and the condition
s>3/r+1
.
We continue by proving the second item. Let
(p,u,\eta)\in\mathcal{O}_{s,r}(\varrho)
be a solution generated by the data
(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\in\mathscr{W}_{s}
. We set
v:\Omega[\eta]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
q:\Omega[\eta]\to\mathbb{R}
via
v=(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}^{-1}
and
q=p\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}^{-1}
. Proposition
7.1
verifies that the map
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}:\Omega\to\Omega[\eta]
is a smooth diffeomorphism that is sufficiently regular up to the boundary as to preserve the notion of classical solution. It is then elementary to verify that
(q,v,\eta)
classically solve (
1.8
) with wave speed
\gamma
, physical parameters
(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}
, and stress-force data
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})

The third and fourth items items are just particular ‘restrictions’ of the map in (
7.45
) from Theorem
7.13
, so long as we define

(7.53)

W_{s}(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa)=\{{(\gamma,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\;:\;(\gamma,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\in W_{s}}\}

and

(7.54)

Z_{s}=\{{(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\;:\;(0,\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})\in W_{s}}\},

and define the mappings (
7.51
) and (
7.52
) via (
7.45
) and the ‘slice’ identifications
W_{s}(\mathfrak{g},\mu,\kappa),Z_{s}\subset W_{s}
.
∎
